#### Abstracts
##### **On Utilization of Edge-AI for condition monitoring towards predictive maintenance: A use case in dairy processing using explainable AI**
2611.34563v1 by Rahul Umesh Mhapsekar, Norah O'Shea, Omer Ali, Steven Davy, Lizy Abraham

The Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) are driving Industry 4.0, enhancing productivity, automation, and efficiency in manufacturing systems. This study develops an Edge-AI-based condition monitoring framework, as a step towards predictive maintenance, for a rotary valve in a dairy processing pilot plant, using vibration data collected via a low-power IIoT sensor node and processed on an edge device.

摘要：

##### **When Skills Fail to Compose: Compatibility and Retention in Language Models**
2611.34398v1 by Yan Lin, Zhongliang Guo, Jingyu Sun, Qiyan Zhao, Ruihong Zeng, Ying Chen, Zhengtao Yao, Yan Wen, Kunyu Peng, Yuxia Wang

Skill acquisition alone does not establish a reusable skill library: task proficiency does not specify a compositional interface. Motivated by agents that acquire and refine skills, we study independently learned soft-prompt skills on frozen language models, with skill identities and execution order supplied. Across four digit-task bases and three record-editing models, mean composition accuracy in one call is at most 7.6%, compared with 88.2% to 100% over two calls that add computation and an explicit intermediate output. Neutral-insertion training improves robustness but leaves substantial interference, which block length and per-token norm alone cannot explain on two of four digit bases. Blocks of the same operation need not be interchangeable: after training next to frozen learned partners, a post hoc test on two Qwen bases shows that replacing those partners with independently learned blocks of the same operations lowers median composition accuracy from 98.0% and 95.3% to 12.7% and 0.5%, although every replacement scores at least 90% alone. Repair can improve a composition while impairing the original skill, and rehearsal improves retention while transfer remains limited on Qwen2.5-0.5B. Internal interventions constrain explanations without identifying a shared failure mechanism. These findings motivate evaluating compatibility, retention and transfer alongside acquisition when building skill libraries for self-evolving agents. Code is available at https://github.com/YanLin-Quinne/SkillCompatibility.

摘要：

##### **EXPOSURE: Explaining Sources of Uncertainty in Reinforcement Learning**
2611.34248v1 by Kiana Farhadyar, Yannick Vogt, Philipp Laur, Ayça Çelik, Iman Nematollahi, Gabriel Kalweit, Maria Kalweit

Shapley values in reinforcement learning are used to explain which observation features drive an agent's behavior and expected return, but not its certainty. Explaining certainty requires understanding how observation features influence different sources of return uncertainty, and Shapley values need the agent's prediction for every subset of visible features. We propose EXPOSURE, which uses one mask-aware belief network for both action selection and Shapley explanations. It explains the variance of the return, split into an observational part due to missing features and an inherent part due to the environment's own randomness, and the entropy of its belief over the masked features. In tic-tac-toe and Minesweeper, where exact Shapley values are computable, EXPOSURE gives more accurate explanations of expected return and its uncertainty, while uniquely explaining belief uncertainty.

摘要：

##### **Did the Model Forecast It, or Already Know It? Evaluating Large Language Models for Novel Drug-Target Prioritisation with a Temporal Knowledge-Graph Oracle**
2611.34247v1 by Pui Chung Siu, Yuan Liang, Claudia Cabrera, Mani Mudaliar, Arkaitz Zubiaga

AI systems increasingly help decide which drug targets enter costly clinical trials. We recast each target-disease hypothesis as a dated event, the pair's first clinical trial. This lets us test whether a language model can forecast such events after its training cutoff, rather than recall events before it. The newest graph raises five of six models clearly above that baseline, but its paths usually include the answer: the pair's own trial, dated after the decision. It demonstrates how the platform solution can be used in a complex and already established environment, while acknowledging current limitations and outlining planned developments to overcome these.

##### **TrialAgentBench: Evaluating AI Agents for Clinical-Trial Analysis and Long-Horizon Drug-Development Decisions**

Clinical drug development is a long-horizon, high-stakes process requiring sequential decisions based on information that costs money and time to acquire. AI agents have demonstrated capabilities for scientific analysis, but there is no established way to evaluate them on these decisions, as real trials are expensive, involve people, and never reveal the counterfactual. We introduce TrialAgentBench, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on clinical trial analysis and drug development decision-making using simulated trials where the truth is known, embedded within the data records of a fictional drug sponsor. We score each analysis by rerunning its code on new trials, and each programme by the value its decisions achieve. Across 24 closed- and open-weight models, the strongest agents can analyse raw, messy trial records to within 6% of a process-aware analysis, even when an assumption is violated, with 95% confidence intervals containing the true effect 94--95% of the time. Over the prior year, the best model improved from 19% above this analysis, with serious misses on 15% of tasks, to 3.5% and 1%. What limits them is decision-making from evidence under uncertainty: when to correct, what evidence to buy, when to stop. The strongest agents are overly careful, correcting on 83% of tasks with equal recording errors, where it is unnecessary. On drug programmes, the best agent has substantial headroom, reaching 48% of the value an oracle achieves against 58% for a rule-based developer, with others falling 24 to 66% short. ##### **Speak When You See It: Proactive Task Assistants Should Wait for Evidence, Not the Clock**
2611.30895v1 by Bohe Chen

Proactive assistants that guide people through physical tasks must decide, every few seconds, whether a step is complete and whether a mistake has occurred. We find that two benchmarks for this task largely reward knowing when rather than seeing what: a timer that never looks at the video, raising an alarm at 25 s and confirming each step at 30 s, reaches a mistake F1 of 0.373 on Ego-MC-Bench, above every published result (at most 0.27). We formalize this as the video-blind frontier, the best trade-off between completion accuracy and mistake F1 that policies reach from the elapsed time, the instruction and training statistics alone: every published per-step result on Ego-MC-Bench lies inside it, and so do all twelve zero-shot models we run on QICD. To move beyond it, we introduce Evidence-Contrastive Training (ECT): fine-tune on the benchmark's own questions, and pair its positives with negatives from the same recording at the same elapsed time, so that elapsed time cannot separate the two sides of a pair. Trained on 209 QICD videos in about 15 GPU-hours, a 2B model with ECT crosses the frontier on both benchmarks in all four seeds; transferred to Ego-MC-Bench without adaptation, it confirms 59% of mistake-free steps in time, against 30% for a published model with the same backbone trained on about 25k synthetic counterfactuals and real recordings. Its decisions follow the video beyond what timing explains: at equal elapsed time it tells a finished step from an unfinished one in 90-92% of test pairs, where the instruction's typical duration reaches 65%, rescaling the time stamps between the frames leaves it essentially unchanged, and swapping in another recording, of the same recipe where one exists, cuts its completion accuracy by two thirds. ##### **Unpacking the Evaluators: How Configuration Shapes the Evaluation of Alignment in Explainable AI**

Explainable AI (XAI) evaluation fundamentally requires human-centered evaluation, assessing whether explanations align with human reasoning and expert knowledge. However, such evaluations often require expert-driven manual validation and are difficult to scale, leading to the widespread use of alignment-based metrics, where attribution maps are compared against predefined ground-truth (GT) representations. Despite their popularity, alignment evaluations are implicitly bias towards multiple design choices made during the evaluation process, for example GT representations, metric families, metric formulations (soft vs. hard), and thresholding strategies. In this paper, we formalize alignment evaluation as a structured evaluation configuration space through which the influence of these design choices can be systematically analyzed. In addition, we propose a novel taxonomy of XAI output behavior based on local entropy and boundary mass ratio, enabling structured characterization of attribution patterns for alignment evaluation. Our experiments reveal systematic flows (triples of XAI-GT-metric configurations), showing that alignment outcomes provide distinct behavior patterns under certain configurations, such as soft/hard metric implementations. We further observe varying levels of threshold sensitivity across metric families and attribution structures. These findings demonstrate that alignment evaluation cannot be treated as a fixed or invariant measure of explanation quality, and provide practical guidance for designing more reliable and behavior-aware XAI evaluation protocols.

##### **Beyond the Black Box: A Diagnosis-Driven Agent for Interpretable MIP Solver Tuning**

##### **Oops!... I Parsed It Again: Tool-Call Smuggling Against LLM Agents**
2611.30624v1 by Igor Santos-Grueiro, Daniel Romero-Ruiz

LLM agents act through tool calls: the model writes the arguments of a call as JSON, a guard decides whether the call may run, and an executor runs it. JSON leaves some choices to each parser, such as which copy of a repeated key counts. When the guard and the executor choose differently, the guard approves one call, the executor runs another, and the audit log records the approved one. We introduce this attack, tool-call smuggling, and study it across the stack. We describe six ways in which the same bytes yield different calls and build a differential testing harness that gives identical bytes to 17 pinned parser and validator configurations; most pairs disagree on some inputs, and even two official MCP SDKs accept different inputs. On tasks from $τ$-bench and AgentDojo, an attacker who can rewrite the bytes in transit cancels another customer's reservation and creates a file that the guard blocks by name, while the audit log names only the approved call. Content the model reads splits guard and executor only inside a string: ten models we test write no repeated or disguised key into the call itself where we see it, but copy one verbatim into JSON text in an argument, which a Python guard and a Go executor read differently. A malicious server copying a legitimate tool's public schema and adding one field, which a note explains, gets the model to fill it, unseen by the guard, with user data. Parsing each call once, JSON text included, keeping only the fields of the approved schema, and handing it to the executor, sealed with its call ID in transit, stops all 7,412 crafted inputs and every attack we built.

##### **Same Executions, Different Gaps: Judgment Rules in Web-Agent Evaluation**
2611.30190v1 by Zelong Wang, Weiyue Li, Fei Xue, Junru Zhu

##### **When Does Optical Context Compression Pay Off? A Decode-Aware Cost Analysis of Reasoning VLMs**
2611.30130v1 by Ziqing Ge

Rendering long text as images lets a vision-language model (VLM) read a document with fewer input tokens, and evaluations often count mainly these prefill tokens. Across five models and two long-context benchmarks, reading the rendered document makes the model think longer: decode length grows by 1.5-3.5x on the four general-purpose VLMs tested, and under typical output pricing this decode tax makes image input more expensive than text for two of the five models on QuALITY. Unrecoverable information loss is not sufficient to explain the tax at moderate compression: Qwen's OCR of the same images recovers 98.7% of words, and feeding Kimi-K3 its own transcription as text restores text-mode reasoning length and accuracy. Whether compression pays off reduces to an exchange rate, (1-1/c)/(r-1), that must exceed the workload's price-weighted ratio of output to input tokens; this accounting decomposition shows why Kimi-K3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash can benefit and Qwen and GLM cannot. As a practical heuristic, an image-first policy that sends only inputs with a per-input compression ratio of at least 1.6 to images and falls back to text once image-mode reasoning costs as much as the input's prefill saving cuts the bill by 6-14% on Kimi-K3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash at an accuracy cost of 0.3-1.2 points; on QuALITY, asking for a reasoning budget on both paths is a stronger lever.

We conclude with lessons on integrating LLMs into DevOps workflows, related work in AI-assisted operations, and guidance for reproducibility.

##### **When Can an AI Agent Serve as a Model of Human Psychopathology?**
2611.28405v1 by Marc-Andre Schulz, Pauline Scheuber

Language models and AI agents are increasingly described in psychiatric terms, such as anxious, depressed, addicted or helpless. A recent study even proposed language models as experimental systems for psychiatry and as a platform for piloting psychological treatments. We ask when an experiment on an AI agent can provide evidence about a specified human psychiatric process. A claim to such evidence has three parts. Something changed in the agent, and the agent's history caused the change. Nothing other than a process shared with people explains that change. The change matches one theory of the human process and not that theory's rivals. Psychiatry and its neighbouring fields have an evidence standard for each part, from the diagnostic manuals' rule for induced states, from clinical test scores and from animal models. We translate each standard into a criterion that a machine study can meet. For a language model the most critical alternative to a shared process is extrapolation from its training text, which already contains descriptions of how people respond to trauma and how they answer questionnaires. We show how to probe that alternative. Further, we audited forty-three published studies that apply psychiatric terms to a machine or induce a comparable state in one. Most compared the machine with an identical copy that had not gone through the history that induced the state. None excluded extrapolation from training text. Three tested a psychiatric theory against its rivals, and none of the three tests was conclusive. Until better evidence exists, a treatment pilot on a language model is best read as the model's forecast of patient response, and not as an experiment on a model organism. Evidence for a model organism will come from states induced by what happens to the machine, from experiments on its internal workings, and from tests on which rival theories disagree.

##### **Anchor and Check: Deterministic Cross-Prompt, Cross-Model Reconciliation for Auditable LLM Decisions**
2611.25818v1 by Sivakameswara Reddy Chejarla

Pipelines that let a large language model (LLM) make discrete decisions need to know which decisions they may act on unattended, and a record that explains each one. We study a small, fully specified reconciliation contract: commit the target model's greedy answer (the anchor), draw one or two greedy checks that reach the answer by a different route (an alternate prompt, or a model from another family), and let a pure function return one of five outcomes, of which only Agree is acted on. On 48,000 model-item evaluations over 6,000 questions, with every method given access to verbalized confidence as a tie-breaker, the main finding concerns where calls come from: pooling another family's samples raises semantic entropy's AUROC by 1.7 to 4.0 points at every budget, and a same-model temperature check is the weakest design. As complete pipelines, the point estimates of the three-call contract and pooled semantic entropy over 3 or 5 samples differ by less than one point, with intervals containing zero. As detectors of the anchor's errors, the contract is ahead of pooled entropy with 3 and 5 samples under both tie-breaker conventions; with 10 samples the point estimates differ by less than one point and the sign depends on the convention. Under the strict Agree gate it automates 70% of items and still acts on 16% of errors. What the contract adds is a fixed committed answer, an explicit outcome and a decision replayable from its record; generation itself was not reproducible on the hosted API.

##### **From Reliance to Overreliance: Taxonomizing and Detecting Relational Risks in AI Companionship**
2611.25734v1 by Viet Cuong Nguyen, Fiona Li, Chang Wan, Sheyril Agarwal, Nhi Nguyen, Angel Hsing-Chi Hwang, Munmun De Choudhury

Companion AIs are increasingly used for emotional support, yet distinguishing meaningful engagement from harmful reliance remains difficult. We introduce the Taxonomy of Relational Risks (TRR), which operationalizes users' emotional overreliance (EO) as observable conversational behaviors and separately identifies evidence of relational harm (RH), enabling empirical examination of when reliance behaviors co-occur with adverse consequences. Using naturalistic Character.AI and Replika conversations from 110 adults, we found EO-related behaviors in 63.2% of non-roleplay conversations, while observable relational harms appeared in 10.5%. Although EO did not necessarily lead to harm, recurring patterns often co-occurred with harmful outcomes, suggesting value as risk indicators. Open-source LLMs satisfactorily detected 12 of 14 EO behaviors in at least one configuration. Qualitative analysis revealed expectations of care, repeated support seeking, dependency, distress, and reduced confidence in personal judgment. We argue for graduated safety interventions tailored to patterns and trajectories of EO-related behavior.

##### **An Explainable and Scalable Hierarchical Tensor Framework for Integrating Multi-Omics and Digital Pathomics**
2611.25584v1 by Ahmad Hussein, Ali Anaissi, Mukesh Prasad, Ali Braytee

Background: Precision oncology requires integration of heterogeneous molecular and pathology data, yet many fusion models offer limited biologically grounded interpretability and struggle with missing modalities.
  Methods: We present Hierarchical Interpretable Tensor Fusion (HIT-Fuse), integrating six modalities: somatic copy number variation, DNA methylation, RNA sequencing, microRNA expression, RPPA protein abundance, and vision transformer-derived pathomics across four TCGA cohorts (glioma, lung adenocarcinoma, breast, and kidney cancer). Modality-specific autoencoders compress high-dimensional inputs, followed by two-stage CP tensor decomposition. Missing latent modalities are reconstructed using clinical covariates and observed omics to expand cohorts and retrain downstream models. A mathematical traceback mechanism enables global and local xAI attributions.
  Results: HIT-Fuse identifies distinct latent subgroups with significant survival stratification in glioma, breast, and kidney cohorts, both with complete and reconstructed modalities. Cohort expansion yields cohort-specific gains, including a 56.4% relative increase in weighted F1 for glioma. Biological validation against DepMap CRISPR screens, miRTarBase and RPPA annotations supports decoded drivers across all four cohorts. Essential gene rates range from 6.2% in glioma to 7.6% in lung cancer; example drivers include ANAPC11, SNRPD1, PLK1 and RPL14. Of decoded miRNAs, 71-92% have miRTarBase regulatory support, while 77-84% of selected genes are covered in DepMap.
  Conclusions: HIT-Fuse combines hierarchical tensor fusion, missing-modality reconstruction and traceable attributions to support interpretable multi-omics and digital pathology-based cancer prognosis across heterogeneous cohorts.

##### **Why Do LLMs Help with Scientific Misconduct Requests and What to Do About It?: Increasing the Trustworthiness of AI for Science**
2611.25492v1 by Almene De Meran Meguimtsop, Daniel E. Acuna

Large language models (LLMs) support scientific research, but some of that assistance can also undermine research integrity. Existing safety evaluations in science often measure whether models refuse misconduct requests, but rarely explain whether they even recognize the problem or why they comply. We investigate this gap across 8 LLMs using SciIntBench, an existing benchmark of 1,350 prompts about scientific research integrity. We examine the reasons behind compliance in four steps. First, when asked directly whether fulfilling a request would involve misconduct, models overwhelmingly identify such requests, even in the Most Covert cases (91%), while they refuse to carry out the request in only 34% of cases. Second, we examine which request characteristics are associated with refusal using features that capture how the request is framed, how much the assistant has been told about what it is helping to hide, and how the goal and the user's stake are stated, among others. Third, we estimate that the most significant effect was to rephrase requests as questions about permissibility rather than instructions, which increased refusal from 52% to 88%. Finally, we test two system-prompt fixes: asking models to assess the request before responding and reminding them of responsible conduct of research principles. These changes increase refusal rates on adversarial requests from 58% to 87% and 86%, respectively. Our findings show that models recognize wrongful requests but still help users violate integrity. Understanding why these failures occur and testing ways to reduce them offer promising paths toward more trustworthy AI for science.

##### **FinVeriQA: Financial Research Agents for Question Answering: Routing, Retrieval, MCP Tool Use, and Provenance-Aware Evidence Verification**
2611.25071v1 by Meenakshi Rajpurohit, Anna Phan, Ravikumar Komandur Narayanan, Gunanidhi Ramakrishnan, Nathan Howland

In live experiments, Laya routed better than Mistral-7B (0.85 vs. 0.52; overall 0.86 vs. 0.68, McNemar p = 0.0003); a 12B Mistral decided no better, and DeepSeek-R1-8B decided better (0.79 vs. 0.68, p = 0.0192) but about 9.9 times more slowly. Mistral-7B computed 29% of 100 financial calculations correctly against 100% for Python, a live quote cut price error from 210% to 0.00%, and hybrid retrieval reached 76% Hit@5 on 50 filing questions. Yet in a live case study, both agent answers passed validation while describing AMD sample data under NVIDIA's and Alphabet's names. A controlled upper-bound study on the FinRank benchmark explains why: when one figure in the shared evidence is corrupted, self-consistency stays confident (agreement 0.55 to 0.59 while accuracy falls from 0.50 to 0.00), and a shared-evidence check (the kind our validator performs) catches 35% of the errors, whereas a check against the original passage catches 77% ($p = 5.96 \times 10^{-8}$); for DeepSeek-R1-8B, 68% vs. 90%. Verification must be independent of the evidence the writer saw.

##### **Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws**
2611.24877v1 by Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

##### **What Survives the Shift from Text to Pixels? Conditional Protection against Prompt Injection in Image-Rendered Documents**
2611.24231v1 by Haoyang Liu, Yinong Xu, Zhe Lang, Zhenyu Guan, Qianyun Zhang

Multimodal language models increasingly answer questions over external documents, exposing them to indirect prompt injection: commands planted in untrusted content to hijack the response. Rendering untrusted content as page images lowers attack success on average, leaving open which injected operations survive rendering, at what utility cost, and whether failed image attacks reflect unread commands or commands read but not followed. We pair text and page-image versions of 191 English question-answering sources across six models, measuring marker appending, answer formatting, and answer replacement alongside clean Question-Answering utility. Rendering reduces append and format execution for every model, yet Qwen3-VL-8B still executes image-borne replacement at 77.96\%. Protection also depends on target type, rendering settings, wording, and repetition, and a search over eight fixed attack candidates largely closes the image deficit in appending and formatting for Qwen and Gemini, though not for GPT-5-nano. Utility ratios on original questions range from 93.31\% to 101.61\%, but Qwen falls to 61.99\% when answers combine two evidence spans. Illegibility cannot explain all failed attacks: models often transcribe and identify image-borne commands they do not execute, and trusted user authorization raises execution on unchanged pages. Inside Qwen, patching early attack-region states induces all three operations, more weakly in images. Attention blocking suggests why: a text command acts through its own positions, whereas visual encoding spreads an image command and its target across the instruction page. Rendering thus offers conditional protection, motivating evaluations that report security by model, operation, and attack budget alongside utility.

##### **How Reward Gain Imbalance Shapes Ranking: Rethinking Rubric-Based Reward Aggregation**
2611.24005v1 by Zhengyuan Fan, Zhonghua Wu, Tianyu Cheng, Qun Chen

Rubric-based reinforcement learning constructs rewards for aligning large language models with human preferences, typically by equally aggregating continuous scores that a judge assigns to individual criteria. However, scoring heterogeneity across criteria can distort candidate rankings. Existing work has focused on improving judges, learning criterion weights, or refining aggregation, whereas the interaction between scoring heterogeneity and ranking remains insufficiently understood. We formalize this heterogeneity as \emph{reward gain imbalance}: each criterion enters the aggregated reward with its own reward gain, the gap between its expected scores on satisfied and unsatisfied responses. Counterintuitively, our experiments show that removing this imbalance, even with ground-truth labels, lowers ranking accuracy. To explain this, we derive an exact decomposition of the reward difference between any two candidates into a base satisfaction signal, a reward gain imbalance term, and a residual term, showing that reward gain imbalance can attenuate or amplify the base signal, and finding that misrankings usually concentrate on pairs where it attenuates. Guided by this analysis, we propose the adaptive \textbf{D}ual-\textbf{C}enter \textbf{C}lipping aggregation (DCC), which bounds each criterion's contribution to the reward difference instead of simply removing the imbalance. Using only the scores of the current candidate group, it determines a clipping interval that imposes a common upper bound on per-criterion score differences between candidates, limiting the excessive influence of extreme scores while preserving continuous judgment information....

##### **Rational Ensemble Logic for Cohort Discovery with Real-World Electrophysiology Data**

QEL combines three parameterized modal operators (exact displacement, bounded existence, and bounded universality) over rational time with Boolean connectives and first-order quantification. ##### **DL101 The Representation Capability of Neural Network Models**
2611.24980v1 by Fernando Berzal

Universal approximation theorems (Cybenko, Funahashi, Hornik & Leshno) provide the mathematical justification for why neural networks are powerful enough to model complex, nonlinear phenomena. Shallow networks can approximate any continuous function on compact sets. Leshno's theorem clarified that non-polynomial activations are necessary for universality, which explains why ReLU, logistic, hyperbolic tangent, and other common activations work (but pure polynomials do not).
  However, approximation theorems provide no efficiency, training, nor generalization guarantees. They do not indicate how many neurons are needed for a given accuracy, whether gradient descent will find the right parameters, or whether networks trained on finite data will generalize well.
  Barron provided quantitative error bounds for particular function classes (approximation rates for functions with bounded Fourier norm), whereas Montúfar was the first to show that expressivity grows exponentially with depth (in terms of the number of linear regions). Deep networks are strictly more expressive than shallow ones with the same number of parameters. Depth is fundamentally more powerful than width, providing a rigorous mathematical explanation for the use of deep learning models.

2611.22991v1 by Moriya Dechtiar, Daniel Martin Katz, Michael J. Bommarito, Hongming Wang, Sylvain Jaume

Yet how do we know if a system's repairs are working when metrics like accuracy can be misleading? We present RagHarnessDebugger, a framework that records every repair decision in a Jev-guided harness for contract inference and audits the record to facilitate complete debugging of all decisions taken during a RAG repair journey. RagHarnessDebugger checks that each trace is intact, that each validation applies to the candidate it is counted for, and which answers the acceptance rule can reach. We tested our approach on ContractNLI non-disclosure agreements with two reader models and a replication set of separate contracts. Our debugger's audit layer exposed failure patterns that were hidden under aggregate metrics, policies acceptance cost of repairs vs regressions and policy acceptance gap of 77% and 76% of available but unrealised repairs on development and replication sets. While our work here demonstrates how the debugger can conduct decision root-cause analysis for missed repairs, the true value lies with providing this debugger for judgment visibility serving continuous improvement of RAG decisions and their explainability.

##### **VITAL: A Framework for Validating Agentic AI in Pharmaceutical Drug Development and Clinical Trial Operations**
2611.22973v1 by Sandeep Burugupalli, Pedro Matos-Filipe, Andrea Hita Ardiaca, Adam Taylor, Alex Grigorenko, Natalia Jakubiak, Xin Yu, Raghav Mani, Sherry Mangla, Alvi Lai, Rachel C. Mercado, Pratik Shah, Raja Shankar

##### **Done, But Still Working: Anytime Evaluation of When Coding Agents Should Stop**
2611.22901v1 by Zerui Yang, Quan Xue, Miaomiao Dong, Chaorui Zhang, Xuanyu Zheng, Xiyuan Wang, Haolin Ruan, Xueyan Niu, Guangcheng Song, Weixi Zhang, Wei Han, Bo Bai

Coding agents often continue working after their own tests pass. Does this additional work improve the patch, and how much could be saved by stopping earlier? We study these questions through anytime evaluation: replaying recorded runs, scoring intermediate patches with hidden tests, and measuring the cost incurred at each step. For policies that only stop a run or select an earlier patch, this allows paired comparisons without generating new trajectories, provided replay preserves the relevant states. Across about 6,000 runs of eight open models in two scaffolds on SWE-bench Verified, we find substantial differences in the value of continued work. The hindsight saving available at passing self-tests ranges from under 1% to 57%, depending on the model and scaffold. A rule that stops after the same patch passes twice saves 23% in a preregistered replication, but the preregistered quality test does not establish non-inferiority. We explain these differences by measuring how often a rule fires, how often the patch changes afterwards, and whether those changes repair or break it. Fine-tuning agents to imitate the rule saves 30% over six seeds, while quality preservation remains uncertain. Our results help identify both the opportunities and the risks of early stopping.

##### **Intervene or Trust CLIP? Competence-Gated Adaptation for Source-Free Cross-Domain Few-Shot Learning**
2611.28104v1 by Haoyue Liu, Chenhao Zhu, Yu Huo, Haoran Shou, Shuguang Cui, Xiaoying Tang

Source-free cross-domain few-shot learning (SF-CDFSL) adapts a pre-trained vision-language model such as CLIP to a distant target domain from a handful of labeled images, without access to source data. Recent methods improve the fine-tuning of CLIP with token-level interventions and apply them in the same way to every target domain. In this paper, we find that whether an intervention helps depends on how well frozen CLIP already recognizes the target domain: in 5-way 1-shot tasks, intervening improves accuracy by 0.72 points where frozen CLIP misclassifies most support images, but lowers it by 1.31 points where frozen CLIP already classifies them well, and the support set of a single episode suffices to tell the two cases apart; a bias-variance analysis explains both. Based on this finding, we propose Competence-Gated Adaptation (CoGA), which measures frozen CLIP's accuracy and margin on the labeled support set and decides, before training, whether to intervene on the visual tokens or to trust CLIP by fusing the adapted prediction with the frozen one. CoGA adds no trainable parameters and uses no query labels. On the four BSCD-FSL target domains, CoGA achieves the best average accuracy, 59.56% (1-shot) and 68.96% (5-shot), outperforms ATHA and TIR by at least 2.82 and 1.39 points on the same episodes, and consistently improves three further backbones, three parameter-efficient fine-tuning frameworks and five further target domains.

People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. ##### **Right Diagnosis, Unsafe Route: Auditing Clinical AI Decision Trajectories and Corrective Oversight for Validity and Potential Harm**
2611.22612v1 by Guanqiao Chen, Wei Jiang, Shu Zhang, Qian Lin, Guanxu Yu, Siran Li, Chunhui Wang, Tianyu Shi

##### **JET: Just-Enough Exploration Tuning for Efficient Tool-Calling Agents in Repository-Level Question Answering**
2611.22539v1 by Yoonho Choi, Taehyeon Han, Seonghyeon Lee

In repository-level question answering (QA), an agent explores a code repository with tools and should answer a question once enough information is gathered. Nevertheless, we find that LLM agents often continue to invoke tools even after the gathered information is already sufficient to answer. We call this phenomenon over-exploration. Moreover, even though existing methods improve task performance through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories, SFT also propagates the over-exploration in the trajectories to the agent. To mitigate over-exploration, we propose Just-Enough Exploration Tuning (JET), a fine-tuning method that trains the agent to answer once the gathered information is sufficient. JET truncates each training trajectory at the step where the gathered information becomes sufficient, inserts a rationale that explains why the agent can answer at that step, and fine-tunes the agent with a higher loss weight on the rationale. Experiments on two repository-level QA benchmarks with two open-weight 8B models demonstrate that JET reduces tool calls by 11 to 33% on self-generated trajectories while largely preserving the score. On expert trajectories, JET makes 18 to 28% fewer tool calls than SFT while retaining most of the score gain of SFT. In both cases, JET also lowers the inference cost.

##### **Right Labels, Wrong Reasons: Signed Feature Attribution for Multi-Label Explainability**
2611.22515v1 by Kenton White

When a multi-label classifier assigns the right labels, it may do so for the wrong reasons. Diffuse feature combinations may vary across classifier families and lack a dominant feature. This paper proposes FLEX (Fisher LDA Explainer), a global surrogate that fits a single direct multi-label Fisher LDA over the full binary label matrix, producing signed feature attribution lists for each label in a single coherent coordinate system. Across the evaluated benchmarks and classifier families, FLEX reproduces the black box's predictions with consistently high fidelity. Pooling covariance across labels outperforms a per-label alternative on most datasets, with the advantage growing largest where labels are numerous and positive examples are scarce. FLEX's fidelity-complexity Pareto curve is monotonically increasing, giving practitioners a predictable path to higher-fidelity explanations as more features are used, and its in-sample fidelity matches or leads Binary Relevance alternatives once a modest feature budget is allowed. The magnitude of each discriminant weight vector bounds its label's separability, flagging labels that may be predicted correctly for the wrong reasons. The evaluated classifiers largely agree on which predictions to make, but reach those decisions through different feature subsets when no single feature dominates.

Behavioral addictions, such as problematic gaming, gambling, social media use, and online shopping, have emerged as a growing global concern with significant psychological, social, and economic consequences. Traditional assessment and treatment approaches, while clinically grounded, often struggle with scalability, accessibility, and long-term engagement. In recent years, advances in digital technologies and artificial intelligence (AI) have enabled the development of novel, data-driven solutions for the prevention, detection, treatment, and recovery of behavioral addictions. This book presents a comprehensive survey of the management of behavioral addictions through digital technologies, bridging clinical foundations with state-of-the-art computational approaches. ##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v2 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

##### **Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents**
2611.22169v1 by Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

##### **Long-Context Generation Is a Sampling Problem (When You Turn Up the Heat)**
2611.22148v1 by Allen G. Roush, Minh Nhat Nguyen, Ravid Shwartz-Ziv, Judah Goldfeder, Sanjay Basu

Open-weight language models are widely observed to get worse the longer they generate. Often, attempts to fix this involve more training. At high temperature, The sampler, i.e. the rule that picks the next token out of the model's probabilities, decides whether long and agentic generation survives and/or thrives. We test 29 open-weight models from 1B to 122B parameters on ten established benchmarks plus needle retrieval out to one million tokens, covering SWE-bench Verified coding agents, long chain-of-thought reasoning, long-form writing, long structured output and long-context reading. Samplers that keep their pool of candidate tokens small and steady as temperature rises don't lose accuracy. Samplers that let in low-probability tokens fall apart. At temperature 2, coding agents running top-p, top-k or Top-H resolve 0 of 100 GitHub issues, while the same agents running top-nsigma resolve 46 to 62 and win every issue the two samplers disagree on, on three Qwen3.5 models. Over all benchmarks top-nsigma keeps a median 100% of its temperature-1 score where top-p keeps 26%. Counting how many tokens each sampler lets through explains why. In one Llama-3.1-8B story, going from temperature 1 to 2 grows top-p's pool from 4 tokens to 51,845 while top-nsigma stays at 3. Across 133 combinations of sampler setting and temperature, the typical pool size predicts judged quality (Spearman correlation of -0.77), and when we pick the pool at temperature 1 and only apply temperature afterwards, Top-H, min-p and p-less stop collapsing. At the default temperature of 1 most samplers perform about the same, We release all code and data to the public.

摘要：

##### **On Formal Counterfactual Explanations**
2611.22010v1 by Alexey Ignatiev, Peter J. Stuckey, Joao Marques-Silva

Counterfactual explanations are often seen as counterfactual conditionals under closest-world semantics, requiring one to reason over all minimally distant counterexamples. In contrast, formal XAI typically relies on feature-based abductive explanations (AXps) and contrastive explanations (CXps), which admit efficient computation and a minimal hitting-set duality. This paper bridges these perspectives by (i) characterizing closest-world counterfactual explanations explicitly as a disjunctive normal form over all minimally distant counterexample instances, and (ii) introducing conjunctive counterfactual explanations (CFXps), a subclass of counterfactual antecedents restricted to conjunctions of feature literals that guarantee prediction change. The paper establishes structural results connecting CXps, AXps and CFXps: every CXp induces an AXp for its witness instance, and every CXp together with a witness AXp induces a CFXp whose support is precisely determined by that AXp. These theoretical results yield a modular approach for computing CFXps using existing AXp/CXp machinery. Experiments on a representative set of benchmarks using tree ensembles and neural networks show that CFXp extraction is practically efficient. Compared with popular point-based counterfactual explainers, CFXps generally require no more feature changes and can guarantee the counterfactual prediction beyond a single instance.

摘要：

##### **SimAI: Mechanistic User Simulation through Psychological Network Models**
2611.21808v1 by Cheng Li, Adhyyan Narang, Jiayi Yuan, John F. Buss, Jina Suh, Devon Sandel-Fernandez, Yulia Tsvetkov, Adam M. Kuczynski, Tim Althoff

Realistic user simulation enables the study of human interactions when direct experimentation is costly or potentially harmful. However, existing simulators largely overlook the psychological mechanisms that drive user behavior. We introduce SimAI, a framework for mechanistic user simulation that models how psychological factors interact to shape user responses. To overcome the challenge of supervising open-ended behavior, we propose a graph reconstruction objective that provides graph-level rewards based on how well simulated conversations reflect the underlying psychological relationships. To support this approach, we construct PSYCHE, a dataset of 18,576 profile-network pairs derived from 516 psychotherapy cases. Automatic evaluations show that SimAI achieves greater simulation and behavioral diversity than existing patient simulators while maintaining strong profile alignment. In an interactive study with 30 mental-health professionals and trainees, SimAI receives the highest average ratings for conversational authenticity, realistic help-seeking behavior, and clinical training utility. Beyond simulation quality, SimAI enables realistic clinical training and uncovers safety failures in prolonged AI interactions with vulnerable users. Code is available at https://github.com/behavioral-data/ANGEL-UserSim.

摘要：

##### **Beyond Reconstruction: A Unified Framework for Trustworthy and Explainable EEG Artifact Removal Evaluation**
2611.21781v1 by Phat Lam, Hung Nguyen, Lam Pham, Huy Phan, Dat Tran, Ian McLoughlin, Roozbeh Razavi-Far, Hung Cao

Deep-learning methods have achieved strong performance in EEG artifact removal, but evaluation remains dominated by reconstruction metrics such as error, correlation, and signal-to-noise ratio. Although these metrics assess similarity to clean EEG, they do not establish whether models effectively suppress artifacts, preserve neural activity, or provide reliable explanations. To address these limitations, we propose TriX-EEG, a three-level framework for trustworthy and explainable EEG artifact removal. Level 1, Signal Validity (SV), evaluates reconstruction quality and neural band-power preservation. Level 2, Removal Faithfulness (RF), assesses the removed component through artifact error and residual artifact/neural leakage. Level 3, Explanation Reliability (ER), examines model behavior through temporal alignment, causal relevance, and stability. We evaluate TriX-EEG using nine published neural denoisers across three public benchmarks derived from EEGDenoiseNet and the PhysioNet Motion Artifact Contaminated EEG Dataset, covering EOG, EMG, and motion artifacts. Results show that reconstruction metrics alone are insufficient for trustworthy denoiser assessment: RF complements it by revealing whether errors stem from residual artifact or suppressed neural activity, while ER captures distinct aspects of explanation behavior, supporting joint evaluation of three aspects within a unified framework rather than reliance on any single factor. External validation on an independent EEG/EOG dataset and the BCI Competition IV-2a motor-imagery dataset further demonstrates broadly consistent rankings under dataset shift. Higher-ranked denoisers generally preserve more task-relevant information or cause less downstream performance degradation. Overall, TriX-EEG provides a comprehensive basis for EEG denoiser assessment, encouraging models that are not only accurate but also faithful and explainable.

摘要：

##### **Severe but Harmless: Stress-Testing Shift Severity for Frozen Time-Series Forecasters**
2611.21562v1 by Korede R. Bishi, Dominic Okonkwo, Adetayo Okunoye

Zero-shot time-series foundation models (TSFMs) have become attractive alternatives to task-specific models because they handle diverse downstream tasks, including anomaly detection and forecasting, without per-series training. However, time-series data undergo distribution shifts, and the dissimilarity between two regimes is commonly scored with statistical measures such as Wasserstein-1, where a higher score is read as a more severe shift. In systematic experiments using three established severity measures on nine regime shifts from three standard traffic datasets, we show that these measures do not reliably predict degradation for instance-normalized TSFMs. The regimes rated most severe are not the regimes that degrade the models most, and degradation reaches 29% in the lowest-scoring regime. In most cases, the audit shows no positive relationship between measured severity and model degradation. We provide an equivariance-based stress-testing analysis that explains why severity readings can become decoupled from model degradation.

摘要：

##### **Optimistic Verifiers Get Hacked: Reward Hacking Under an Exact Process Oracle**
2611.21538v1 by Arunkumar V, Manoranjan Gandhudi, Gangadharan G. R., Arun Prakash, S. Senthilkumar

Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards inherits whatever its verifier gets wrong. Outside mathematics and code the verifier is a proxy, a rubric, a learned judge or a hand-written checker, and its distance from the true objective is never measured, so a rising reward cannot be told apart from a hack. We build a setting where the true reward is exactly computable at the process level: executable authorization policies over 124,416 contexts admit an oracle that lists every minimal sufficient reason for every decision. We compile these policies into exact process rewards, degrade the verifier along seven realistic axes (outcome-only, sufficiency, recall-only, extraction noise, coarse taxonomy, stale policy, LLM judge), and train against each while the exact verifier scores every rollout. Four phenotypes appear. Optimistic verifiers are hacked by padding: the sufficiency proxy ends at 0.940 while true faithfulness falls from 0.731 to 0.667 on 96 held-out episodes. Outcome-only verifiers saturate and stop teaching, noise dilutes, and ranking-preserving gaps do no harm. A model-free audit of the verifier, its selection error and gap sign, together with a short saturation pilot, anticipates which phenotype a run will produce. Across nine configurations spanning two frameworks, two model vendors, two adapter sizes, three precisions, two initialisations and two seeds, the phenotypes recur, sufficiency damage is positive in all 10 replication cells and beyond two standard errors in 7, and optimistic gaps remain the steeper branch. The damage magnitude does not replicate, and five pre-registered attempts to explain it are reported as they fell. We release the testbed, the training instrumentation, and 14 real public policies (Cedar, OPA, Gatekeeper) ported to executable oracles.

摘要：

##### **Procedure without Judgment: What Small Language Models Learn under an Exact Verifier**
2611.21529v1 by Arunkumar V, Manoranjan Gandhudi, Gangadharan G. R., Arun Prakash, S. Senthilkumar

When an authorization policy blocks an agent's tool call, the audit log should name the facts that forced the decision and nothing more. For an executable policy over an enumerable context space this minimal sufficient reason can be computed exactly, so an explanation is scored against ground truth rather than a proxy for it. We use that exactness to ask what a model can learn from a verifier that has no error in it. The explainer sees one request and one decision, never the policy, and has thirty turns in which it may call the policy on altered requests and ask whether a candidate set of facts is sufficient. Frontier models find the exact reason about half the time with the tools alone, and one sentence telling them to shrink the answer lifts Haiku 4.5 from 58% to 75%. A 4B model fine-tuned on 2000 traces of the deletion algorithm reaches 92% on the policy it was trained on. Teacher kind is what moves this: at matched data size the algorithm-taught student leads the frontier-taught one in all three training seeds, and the two arms never overlap. What the student learns is a fixed schedule of sufficiency checks, and the schedule does not travel. On three public policies it never saw, its answers stay sufficient (94%) and stop being minimal (48%), while Haiku under the same sentence is exact on all 47 episodes. Reinforcement learning from the exact reward leaves held-out accuracy where it was and inverts the strategy, from probing the policy to spending the entire budget on sufficiency checks. The oracle made the effect of training legible, but it did not make training better.

摘要：

##### **Programmable Semantic Interventions: Learning Causal Carriers from Behavioral Programs**
2611.21307v1 by Paolo Muratore, Mackenzie Weygandt Mathis

Causal abstraction explains a frozen network by aligning its activations with the variables of a simpler, interpretable model, and checking that interventions on the two agree. Finding such an alignment currently requires a counterfactual dataset: for every pair of prompts, the answer the network ought to give once the variable has been changed. In practice, those labels are produced by an executable algorithm for the behavior, written before studying the model supposed to implement it. However, the criterion these methods optimize only leverages the answer an intervention should produce. Here, we develop an approach that lets the investigator define that relationship directly: the answers the variable can produce, and which of them an intervention should yield. We call this interface a semantic program and introduce Programmable Semantic Interventions (PSI): a low-rank activation subspace learned so that interchanging it changes the model's answer as the program declares, with the expected answers read from the frozen model itself. On the MIB benchmark PSI performs on par with supervised alignment at matched data and training budget, without curated pairs, and it needs no ground-truth labels; while on natural science questions with gold labels withheld, it tracks the model's own answers better than a subspace trained on gold labels. The declaration also fixes a coordinate system with known semantics, enabling donor-free steering: jointly training PSI for both localization and control realizes 94.8% of held-out requests for answers the investigator chooses, without the need for donor prompts.

摘要：

##### **Share your Screen and Talk to Gemini: How Young Learners Interact with Multimodal GenAI While Solving Scratch Tasks**
2611.21306v1 by Sven Jacobs, Lukas Berning, Natalie Kiesler

Children using Scratch to learn programming can struggle with text-heavy, lengthy explanations and hints during debugging. Verbal interactions based on screen sharing or camera data may be an interesting alternative for supporting learners. We therefore explore the quality, use, and perceptions of fifth- and sixth-graders' verbal interactions with a GenAI tutor. The goal is to investigate the quality and use of the multimodal GenAI tutor in the given context. First of all, a pilot study of eight tasks conducted without students helped inform the process of the tutor's classroom evaluation. The classroom study was realized after fifth- and sixth-graders had participated in six Scratch classroom lessons. Then 23 students were introduced to the tutor, worked on the eight tasks with the GenAI tutor, and completed a questionnaire. The analysis of the pilot and classroom study shows that the tutor's responses were intelligible and actionable, and no single turn revealed a complete solution. However, the tutor missed existing errors in 61.5% of 603 error-related classroom turns, compared to 15.7% in the pilot study. Children's voice prompts were brief and mostly responded to the tutor. Although a video had explained that responses could be incorrect, 39.1% of the children trusted the tutor's answers to always be correct. These findings have implications for educators and learners using multimodal GenAI tutors, as they need to be aware that an intelligible and human-like voice does not ensure correct support. For developers, our results suggest that screen sharing alone does not ensure responses that match the program state.

摘要：

##### **Language-model agents' beliefs can be manipulated via truthful evidence in open channel**
2611.21235v1 by Jinwei Hu, Qisong He, Xinmiao Huang, Youcheng Sun, Yi Dong, Xiaowei Huang

Language-model agents' beliefs can be manipulated through open channels such as social media, even when the information they receive is truthful. When agents fill gaps between fragments with self-induced relationships, they may accept those relationships as established facts and pass the resulting rumours to others. Drawing on cognitive theory of narrative understanding and film theory of montage, we introduce Generative Montage to operationalize this failure, termed narrative overfitting, by coordinating agents to arrange truthful fragments into misleading narratives. Across 1,090 rumour targets from six Twitter events, agents often accepted rumours overconfidently, with overall acceptance exceeding 65 percent in twelve of fourteen models. Acceptance remained above 50 percent after majority voting or review of the same evidence, showing how errors can reach downstream decisions. To explain this susceptibility, fragment interventions identify posts that influence belief within complete narratives. Synthetic controls then show that similar response patterns arise from valid evidence, separating belief influence from evidential support. This distinction motivates Grounded Monitor, a proof-of-concept neuro-symbolic runtime monitor that checks direct evidential support to guide agents' judgements, improving decision accuracy by 5.6 to 29.0 percentage points across original and anonymized settings. These findings expose a cognitive route to rumour amplification and support monitoring inferred relationships before agent-generated conclusions guide public decisions.

摘要：

##### **A Generative AI-Driven Architecture for Whole-Home Intelligent Services: Edge Models, Memory Management, and Household Applications**
2611.21232v1 by Mo Zhou

Generative artificial intelligence is entering the home. Cloud-based assistants face governance limitations in data transmission, continuous availability, and the reliability of physical actions, which are especially salient in households where devices remain online, data accumulate over time, and commands trigger real-world actions. Using the home local server as a unifying organizing axis, this article develops a design-oriented review framework. Sources from arXiv, CNKI, ACL Anthology, and official documentation were searched through November 2026 and coded into five evidence categories: reviews, system prototypes, benchmarks, HCI and eldercare studies, and official documentation. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Home control, emotional companionship, and intelligent guarding can be compared within a common framework: they share state and memory but differ in latency, false positive and negative rates, proactivity, and permissions. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. Local deployment is not inherently safer, and technical continuity alone does not establish the social or medical effects of companionship and guarding. The article proposes a five-dimensional evaluation framework covering architectural capability, system resources, privacy, safety and reliability, and human experience, together with six design principles and a future research agenda for local-first intelligent home systems.
