#### Abstracts
##### **Spectrum Adaptation of AI-Native Autoencoder Communications with Transfer Learning**
2611.34942v1 by Yalin E. Sagduyu, Tugba Erpek

End-to-end physical-layer learning replaces separate modulation, coding, and detection operations with an autoencoder (AE) that consists of a jointly trained encoder-decoder pair at the transmitter and the receiver. This paper studies AI-native communications with AEs and quantifies the sensitivity to training-test mismatch in channel models. Models trained only on an AWGN channel achieve low bit error rates (BERs) under matched conditions but degrade severely on Rayleigh fading with practical front-end impairments. To reduce the time and energy required to adapt to new channel conditions, we propose a low-latency transfer-learning procedure that starts from an AWGN-pretrained system and fine-tunes only a small subset of transmitter and receiver layers while freezing the remainder. We further formulate layer selection as a budget-constrained optimization problem that identifies the most informative parameters to update during adaptation. This preserves the learned signal geometry and the deep decision structure while adapting lightweight front ends to fading- and impairment-induced mixing. For AE communications, we show that partial updates remove the mismatch-induced error floor and approach the BER of fully retrained models. We provide a layer-wise study and quantify resource-performance trade-offs, showing that modest adaptation substantially reduces BER while cutting training time, multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations, energy consumption, and the fraction of updated parameters for resource-efficient implementation.

摘要：

##### **Can image generation help visual reasoning? An empirical study**
2611.34935v1 by Xueyang Yu, Yifan Jing, Taiming Lu, Linrong Cai, Zhipeng Cai, Yunyang Xiong, Gabriel Sarch, Zhuang Liu

Language models think in words; then multimodal models might think in images. We test whether a generated helper improves the answer to a visual question. A model describes the helper it needs and then answers using the original and generated images together. Across 13 vision-language model and generator pairs, generation lowers mean accuracy by 3.7 percentage points, with declines on nine of ten tasks. Two unified models, BAGEL and Janus-Pro-7B, each lose accuracy on eight tasks. On VSTAR, adding a generated image to the same planning hint lowers accuracy by 10.47 points. Repeated image edits yield different outcomes across tasks. ChartQAPro and MathVista stay below their no-generation baselines, while ERQA gains three points after ten edits.

摘要：

##### **Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces for Hard Math Problems**
2611.34928v1 by Lukas Aichberger, Adam Golinski, Arno Blaas, Arwen Bradley, Jacob Springer, Mehrdad Farajtabar, Michael Kirchhof, Sinead Williamson

Post-training large language models (LLMs) for mathematical reasoning often relies on accepted traces whose final answers pass a verifier. For hard math problems, however, such traces can be rare under independent Monte Carlo (MC) sampling. To address this, we propose Diverse Alternative Reasoning Traces (DART), a trace-mining method that starts from a rejected trace, decomposes it into reasoning steps, and ranks them by position-weighted uncertainty. It then generates deliberately distinct alternative candidates for highly ranked steps using reference-answer guidance and a meta-thought before completing the resulting traces. On greedy-unsolved DeepMath-103K problems, DART recovers 37% more problems than MC sampling at a matched number of generated traces. Its estimated generation cost per recovered problem is nearly identical, while its cost per accepted trace is 30% lower. Post-training Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on traces mined by DART achieves the highest point estimate in five of six evaluations across MATH-500 and AIME benchmarks. These results demonstrate that revisiting rejected traces with DART can expand problem coverage and provide effective post-training data.

摘要：

##### **Mixture-of-Loops Transformer**
2611.34923v1 by Lianghui Zhu, Shijie Wang, Xinggang Wang, Jinze Bai, Bo Wang, Xiang Bai

Looped Transformers boost expressivity by reusing shared layers across loops, yet at the cost of efficiency. Pushing their expressivity-efficiency frontier requires rethinking the core mechanism connecting adjacent loops: What should be passed as a Transformer loops? We introduce Mixture-of-Loops Transformer (MoLT), whose Mixture-of-Loops Attention (MoLA) uses keys and values (KV) as memory for selective reuse across depths and loops. Under loop residual, MoLT-R retains the residual stream and reads local KV across loops for expressivity. Under loop KV, MoLT-KV instead restarts from embeddings and reads current-loop local KV with preceding-loop memory KV at earlier positions for efficiency. MoLT-KV eliminates same-position cross-loop dependence and, paired with loop forcing for parallel training, unlocks single-traversal Mixture-of-Loops (MoL) decoding beyond the training loop horizon. To balance efficiency and quality, we introduce U-shaped MoLT (U-MoLT), which separates memory construction from prediction readout and skips unused decoder traversals without changing predictions. In 400B-token pretraining, U-MoLT outperforms OLMo3 across 700M/1.5B dense and 3B MoE models, achieving 1.7x validation cross-entropy (CE) token efficiency at 700M while improving 3B MoE 10-domain perplexity by 0.75 and 10-task accuracy by 2.1 percentage points. In 100B-token continued pretraining of Qwen3.5-0.8B-Base, our U-MoLT adaptation lowers CE on all 11 domains over the matched baseline with 1.4x mean token efficiency. At inference, U-MoLT with MoL-Hybrid-2 matches non-looped OLMo3 latency at 996.6 vs. 1,000.6 ms on S-size (~113M) models. Code is available at https://github.com/hustvl/MoLT

摘要：

##### **$μ$-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-User Task Completion in Shared Interaction Voice Agents**
2611.34920v1 by Qihao Lin, Guanxu Chen, Shuhang Zheng, Xiaoya Lu, Jingyi Yang, Terumi Chiba, Wenyi Yu, Chao Zhang, Zhuan Shi, Jing Shao

Voice agents in shared spaces must satisfy interdependent multi-user requests while upholding per-user authority, privacy, and task constraints. Existing evaluations offer limited coverage when participants react throughout a session. We introduce $μ$-Bench, a benchmark for joint task completion in closed-loop, full-duplex multi-user voice interaction. Simulated participants act from isolated profiles of goals, knowledge, and permissions, adapting to the agent's speech and tool outcomes. Across 20 domains, 100 scenarios vary group size, acoustics, and follow-up demands to form 800 cases (408 tool-augmented, 392 tool-free). Joint Task Success (JTS) requires all user goals, privacy boundaries, and terminal states to hold jointly, while Pure JTS further disallows unauthorized tool calls; Attribution, Threading, and Coordination (ATC) diagnostics localize failures. Across 13 voice agents, proprietary systems peak at 62.24\% tool-free and 65.44\% tool-augmented success, while open-weight models remain below 20\%. ATC diagnostics show that open-weight models lag primarily in multi-turn attribution and threading, and controlled contrasts reveal that larger groups consistently degrade joint task success across capable systems. By coupling reactive multi-user simulation with verifiable session-level outcomes, $μ$-Bench provides a rigorous testbed for shared voice agents.

摘要：

##### **Enhancing Maritime Domain Awareness using Latent Space Representations**
2611.34899v1 by Rikard Vinge, Miguel A. Belenguer-Plomer, Isabelle Wittmann, Octavian Dumitru, Michele Lazzarini, Sergio Albani, Conrad M Albrecht

This work presents a novel application of Earth Observation (EO) embeddings for Maritime Domain Awareness (MDA) from the perspective of the geospatial foundation model TerraMind. By operating directly on compressed latent-space representations rather than on the original Sentinel-1 imagery, this approach directly addresses the data gravity bottleneck that increasingly constrains operational EO processing, in line with the objectives of the Embed2Scale project. In particular, linear and non-linear probing of compressed embeddings, latent space representations of synthetic aperture radar (SAR) imagery, is used to detect, localise, and characterise vessels from Sentinel-1 GRD images. Vessel detection achieves a ROC-AUC of 0.95, while vessel localisation has a median error of around 300 m for vessels longer than 100 m, and vessel AIS type-code classification achieves 62% accuracy for the majority classes. The latter, however, is highly correlated with vessel length. These results reveal both the potential and current limitations of Geospatial Foundation Models (GeoFMs) when applied to fine-grained MDA understanding in real-world scenarios.
  Additionally, a new maritime remote sensing dataset is released, pairing about 2,000 Sentinel-1 images with temporally aligned AIS signals from commercial vessels. Through manual inspection, each candidate sample was verified for bounding-box quality and for consistency between the AIS-reported vessel length and the observed vessel extent, cross-checking vessel identity and location against publicly available vessel records and, where available, photographs. The dataset was made ready for use with the NeuCo-Bench neural compression benchmark framework, publicly available on HuggingFace through https://huggingface.co/datasets/embed2scale/S1-single-ship-downstream .

摘要：

##### **Interpretable and Accurate Hybrid Decision Trees with Selective Case-Based Splits**
2611.34887v1 by Cristiano Landi, Alessio Cascione, Riccardo Guidotti

Decision trees are among the most well-known interpretable models in Machine Learning. Over the years, a wide variety of strategies have been proposed to define split conditions within the greedy learning framework, aiming to guide instance routing and enhance predictive performance. These strategies range from axis-parallel and oblique splits based on feature values to proximity-based splits using exemplar instances and more complex hybrid approaches. However, no single method has emerged as universally superior. In this work, we present two key contributions to the construction of greedy decision trees. First, we propose a hybrid tree architecture that integrates both feature-based and case-based splits, allowing for heterogeneous splits throughout the tree. Second, we introduce a novel type of split that leverages a selective case-based approach: when a split is needed, our method searches for the most informative subset of features to define the split, effectively balancing the specificity of case-based methods with the simplicity of feature-based ones. Experiments on a diverse set of tabular datasets show that our method outperforms standard decision trees and achieves performance on par with ensemble methods, while producing shallow trees that retain or even improve predictive accuracy.

摘要：

##### **How Much Do Coding Agents Over-Edit, and Why? An Empirical Study of Unneeded Edits in Repository-Level Agent Patches**
2611.34875v1 by Aakash Goel, Benjamin Steenhoek, Yu Hu

Agent patches are larger than developers', which is widely read as over-editing. But patch size does not say whether the extra edits are needed, what they are, or where they come from. We introduce Delta Residue Attribution (DRA), which replays agent trajectories, traces every block (unit) of the final patch to the step that wrote it, reverts each unit, and reruns the task tests to decide whether the unit is needed. We apply DRA to 7,660 resolved GitHub Copilot CLI trajectories from 18 frontier models on SWE-bench Verified (6,728) and DeepSWE (932), and grade developer patches using the same method. Agent patches do change more lines than developers'; on SWE-bench Verified the majority of extra edits add tests (61%) and documentation (13%) while on DeepSWE, most extra edits are code (58%). On both benchmarks, agents' code is on average at least as necessary as developers' (mean $NP_{code}$ 0.887 vs. 0.864 on SWE-bench Verified, where 11 of 18 models leave less unneeded code than developers, and 0.797 vs. 0.785 on DeepSWE). On average, 11% of an agent patch's code units on SWE-bench Verified are still unneeded, and 89% of those change behavior. Debug steps write almost none of the unneeded code. Code necessity differs substantially by model; it is unrelated to resolve rate on SWE-bench Verified and negatively correlated with it on DeepSWE (Spearman -0.59, p = 0.01). On SWE-bench Verified, pruning with DRA using only the tests the agent can see (its own tests and the repository's) removes the majority of unneeded code but also some needed code (67% recall, 46% precision), and 89% of pruned patches still resolve the task; asking the agent to audit its own diff is more precise but less effective (36% recall, 66% precision). We recommend that benchmarks report code necessity next to resolve rate and that over-editing be addressed through model training and harness-level pruning.

摘要：

##### **A Survey of Security, Privacy, and Ethics of Large Language Models in Telecom**
2611.34868v1 by Joy Bose, Srinivas Adyapak

Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into telecommunications for customer service, network analytics, and operational support. While offering efficiency and innovation, their deployment raises critical privacy, security, and ethical challenges due to the uniquely sensitive and longitudinal nature of telecom data. This paper presents a survey of LLM risks and safeguards in telecom context. Our survey introduces a telecom-specific taxonomy linking privacy, security, and ethics with use cases and outlines open research challenges such as longitudinal data privacy, cross-border compliance, and scalable guardrails.

摘要：

##### **RePace: Reasoning about Demonstration Speedup for Faster Vision-Language-Action Policies**
2611.34864v1 by Insup Lee, Hojin Jeon, Sunguk Shin, Seoungbin Bae, Taekwan Kim, Jinwoo Shin, Dongsu Han

Since vision-language-action (VLA) policies can inherit the slow pace of human demonstrations, accelerating these demonstrations before fine-tuning can lead to faster VLA policies without an additional speed-selection module at deployment. Several offline speedup methods have focused on identifying segments that require less precision and assigning predefined speedup factors to the segments. However, even within a single segment, a speedup factor suitable for steady motion may be too high when precise alignment is required. To this end, this paper presents RePace, an offline reasoning framework that determines how much to accelerate each part of a demonstration. For the same recorded movement, RePace measures how much each candidate speedup factor shortens the sequence and compares the resulting observation-action pairs used for training. Given the task instruction, a pretrained language model uses the visual context and original motion of successive windows to interpret these candidate retiming effects and select a speedup profile for the complete episode. Experimental results demonstrate that RePace outperforms the baselines in overall task execution efficiency on both LIBERO and RoboCasa-Kitchen. On all three tasks with a gripper-equipped Franka Research 3 arm, RePace matches or exceeds the acceleration baselines in success rate and completes successful trials faster.

摘要：

##### **BOP-Refer: Benchmark for Monocular 3D and 2D Object Grounding**
2611.34862v1 by Tomas Hodan, Vineet Bhat, Martin Sundermeyer, Jonathan Tremblay, Yash Patel, Médéric Fourmy, Stephen Tyree, Junwen Huang, Varun Burde, Martin Cífka, Eric Brachmann, Vincent Lepetit, Stan Birchfield, Jiri Matas, Carsten Rother

We introduce BOP-Refer, the first benchmark for monocular 3D and 2D grounding of everyday and industrial objects sourced from 9 diverse BOP datasets. We pair images from these datasets with VLM-generated and human-verified referring expressions, and derive accurate ground-truth 3D and 2D bounding boxes of the referred objects from their 3D models and 6DoF pose annotations. Our systematic evaluation of 17 frontier VLMs across the major proprietary and open-weight families (Gemini, GPT, Claude, Qwen, Gemma) shows that the best model, Gemini 4 Carbon, reaches 72.6% AP_IoU2D in 2D but only 12.4% AP_IoU3D in 3D, despite the more lenient IoU thresholds in 3D. The predicted 3D boxes are usually centered on the target objects in the image but often off in depth by more than the object's size, suggesting that metric scale estimation, which needs reliable object size priors, is currently by far the main source of error.

摘要：

##### **From Prediction to Decision: Decision-Focused Fine-Tuning of Autoregressive Tabular Transformers for Cost-Sensitive Financial Events**
2611.34858v1 by Ashkan Golgoon, Giacomo Domeniconi, Samuel Atkins, Sergio Servantez, Zain Khandwala

Autoregressive transformers trained on tokenized tabular event streams are a simple and strong baseline for predicting financial events such as fraud. Yet institutions do not act on probabilities; they act on decisions whose consequences are asymmetric and heteroscedastic: the cost of a missed high-value fraud and that of a wrongly declined small purchase are not the same. The standard bridge is post-hoc Bayes-optimal thresholding of calibrated probabilities under a cost matrix. We ask whether fine-tuning the model to the decision objective can do better. We fine-tune the same pretrained model so that its restricted softmax over label tokens (already a distribution over business actions) maximizes expected utility under an institution-specified, amount-scaled cost matrix. Because the reward is a known function of (action, outcome, amount) and the outcome is observed in the data, the expected-utility objective and its gradient are available in closed form. This is example-dependent cost-sensitive learning whose per-example loss is linear in the predicted probability, with no sampling, critics, or advantage estimation. A KL anchor to the pretrained model and an auxiliary language-modeling term preserve calibration and the generative event model. A sampled, group-normalized (GRPO-style) variant provides the extension point to trajectory-level rewards.

##### **Probing Visual Idiosyncrasies in Text-to-Image Models**
2611.34843v1 by Yongqian Peng, Hongjie Li, Yichao Zhou, Chi Zhang, Yixin Zhu

Text-to-Image (T2I) models turn short text descriptions into images, and the leading models now produce images of comparable visual quality. Whether their outputs still carry model-specific characteristics matters for attribution, for model comparison beyond aggregate quality, and for evaluations that assume anonymity. We ask whether images from frontier T2I models can still be classified by source, and which visual properties make this possible. Here we show that a standard ResNet-50 identifies the source of an image among HiDream-O1-Dev, FLUX.2-dev, HunyuanImage-3.0, and Qwen-Image-2512, the four highest-ranked open-source models on the Text-to-Image Arena, with 100.00% accuracy on held-out prompts. Human participants reach only 62.61% on the pairwise version of this task, so the distinguishing cues are not visually obvious. They are also not tied to a particular classifier and survive severe image corruptions and out-of-distribution prompts. We trace them to visual idiosyncrasies at three levels. Low-level texture statistics alone let a lightweight multi-layer perceptron match ResNet-50. Object layout, depth, and albedo estimated from the images each carry a source signal. Image captions reveal model-specific preferences in style and background that the prompts never specify. A one-sentence hint about these cues raises human accuracy by 9.03 percentage points. These idiosyncrasies make source classification a tool for measuring similarity between models and between real and generated images, and they caution against assuming anonymity in human preference evaluation of T2I models.

##### **Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent: Self-Structuring Learning through Question Answering**
2611.34841v1 by Masahiro Kato

We consider the problem of constructing knowledge bases for applications based on large language models, in which the structure of a knowledge base determines how an application can retrieve and connect the information needed to answer a question. We study Self-Structuring Learning, a setting in which questions and expected answers guide the construction of a persistent knowledge base whose editable structure can be a graph schema or the organization of linked knowledge documents. We propose Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent, which diagnoses discrepancies between expected answers and responses obtained through the current knowledge base. At each round, the method proposes a structural edit, rebuilds the knowledge base, and adopts the edit when the learning loss decreases. The retained structure is evaluated on other questions about the same sources. By randomizing over candidate edits, we express the loss change of an edit as a derivative with respect to the probability of selecting it, and we evaluate joint edits as single candidates because their changes interact. Schema Descent instantiates the method for graph schemas. In experiments on large documents, each a bundle of research papers, the updated knowledge cards and graph schemas obtain higher mean Answer F1 on held-out generated questions than their initial structures, with schema gains in every document. On human questions, mean F1 rises for schemas and falls for cards: the updated structures serve the kind of question they were learned from.

##### **Proverbs As A Window On Collective Prescriptions**
2611.34832v1 by Tomás Lejarraga, Dirk U. Wulff, Ralph Hertwig

Proverbs, as repositories of traditional wisdom, have informed human reasoning for millennia. Yet the collective prescriptions they encode have remained largely unquantified. We introduce a computational framework to analyze nearly 50,000 English-language proverbs. Using large language models, validated against human judgments, we map the topical landscape of proverbs, assess their prescriptive power, and quantify their recommendations on key dimensions of human judgment and behavior. We find that most proverbs are prescriptive, offering guidance in domains under individual control, such as preparation and prudent action, whereas proverbs about uncontrollable circumstances, such as fate and mortality, tend to describe rather than prescribe. When prescriptive proverbs take a consistent stance, they favor caution over risk, long-term over short-term outcomes, honesty over deception, and kindness over malice, and show a moderate tendency toward suspicion over trust. On dimensions involving genuine trade-offs, especially autonomy versus compliance, proverbs provide contradicting advice rather than a single recommendation. This analysis provides a quantitative map of the collective prescriptions in a culture's proverbial wisdom, revealing a core of prudent, honest, and forward-looking guidance alongside varied advice where circumstances dictate the best course of action.

Epistemic uncertainty (EU) is the signal that marks where that experience ends. In this work we offer an extensive empirical study on how training objectives and architectures shape epistemic uncertainty of world models. More precisely, we present a controlled comparison of post-hoc EU (last-layer Laplace and ensembles) between two dominant models of different paradigms: 1) LeWorldModel (LeWM): A joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), which predicts the next embedding without a decoder, and 2) DreamerV3: A recurrent state-space model (RSSM), which reconstructs the observation from a recurrent latent state. In ten simulated visual-control environments, we follow a controlled state perturbation of the environment state through every stage of the models up to the final EU estimate. Its recurrent update averages over context, so a change in a single frame reaches the state at a fraction of its size and recovers only as the change persists. 2611.34627v1 by Dean Ariel, Seffi Cohen

Comparing ensembles, routers and cascades requires the output of every model on every item, and its cost. Stored collections built for routing keep no per-option probabilities. DecisionReplay holds the probability vectors of 17 commercial decision models, endpoints that return a probability for every answer option, on 35 benchmarks, with the token counts, billed cost and measured latency of every call, all released. The models are wrong together: two that are both wrong choose the same wrong option in 72% of cases. ##### **PURA: Video Token Pruning via Unified Spatiotemporal Redundancy Assessment**
2611.34521v1 by Yuyao Sun, Hongli Yuan, Shuang Li, Tao Deng, Deqing Wang

Video large language models incur substantial inference costs from long visual token sequences. Existing video token compression methods mitigate these costs by exploiting spatial redundancy within frames and temporal redundancy across frames. However, many approaches impose predefined processing orders or fixed allocation ratios for spatial and temporal compression, limiting their ability to adapt to the varying balance of spatiotemporal redundancy across videos. To address this limitation, we propose PURA, a training-free method that prunes visual tokens through unified spatiotemporal redundancy assessment. For each token, PURA constructs spatial and temporal reference candidates and selects the reference with the lowest representation error across the two dimensions. The representation error is combined with token importance to determine each token's removal cost. To avoid the time overhead of removing the lowest-cost token one by one, we introduce Coordinated Pruning, which iteratively groups mutually compatible low-cost removals into batches for simultaneous execution and updates the pruning state after each batch until the target budget is reached. ##### **Inverse Reinforcement Learning with State-Dependent Foresight for Human Decision-Making**
2611.34351v1 by Jinil Kim, Jinwoo Jeong, Hyeongjun Yun, Min-hwan Oh, Woo-Young Ahn

Computational modeling of behavior represents individuals with a model fitted to their observed behavior and characterizes them through its parameters. Naturalistic tasks incorporate the complexity of real environments and yield high-dimensional behavioral trajectories. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) infers the latent reward function underlying these trajectories. Standard IRL, however, assumes effectively infinite-horizon planning, whereas human foresight is both bounded and state-dependent. Here we introduce SC-AIRL (State-Conditional Adversarial IRL), which builds these two cognitive properties into the planner. ##### **MeterRouter: Budget-Aware Dynamic Routing for Multi-Agent Systems**

The cost of answering a query is set jointly by how many agents are recruited, the role and LLM assigned to each, and communication topology among them. ##### **RAP: Radial-Angular Random Projection for Hyperbolic Representations**

Random projection is a basic compression primitive for Euclidean representation learning, with the Johnson--Lindenstrauss lemma giving a target dimension $k=O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log n)$ independent of the ambient dimension. Student-side training uses only final-answer cross-entropy on a small subset of task training examples; it requires neither teacher outputs nor intermediate solution traces. We select the student injection layer and intervention scale on a labeled support set and report results on the disjoint remainder of each evaluation split. On GSM8K and MATH, the projected vector improves base students by 10.37-21.98 accuracy points across the evaluated teacher-student settings. On four code-generation benchmarks, the Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct student gains 3.23-4.67 pass@1 points, while the base Qwen2.5-7B student gains up to 9.65 points. These results show that a teacher-side reasoning vector can be reused across smaller students with different residual dimensions through projector-only adaptation.

摘要：

##### **Agentic-Level Adversarial Purification: Revisiting Diffusion Purifiers on Native-Resolution Agentic Inputs**
2611.34181v1 by Yan Lin, Zhongliang Guo, Jingyu Lin, Daizong Liu

Vision-language agents act on native-resolution renders of multi-element pages, where one perturbed photo or a few altered text strokes can redirect the decision. Any content provider can craft such a perturbation on the open model behind the agent, so a plug-in purifier is the natural first line of defense. Yet diffusion purifiers were designed and evaluated at the fixed resolution of the diffusion model on classifier or natural-image benchmarks, and defense evaluations for agents test JPEG, blur and noise but no diffusion purifier. We present the first study of adversarial purification at the agentic level, for an agent built on Qwen3-VL-8B: purifiers run at native resolution on multi-element inputs, face grey-box attackers who know the agent's model but not the defense, and are compared at matched clean cost. We show that treating agentic inputs as classifier inputs misleads both evaluation and design. Resizing the input to the resolution of the diffusion model, as existing purifiers do, raises the TextVQA clean cost of the Divide-and-Conquer purifier (DC) from 4.0 to 35.7 points. On web-shop pages, DC keeps 85.0% grey-box robust accuracy against web-hardened perturbations, where cheap input transformations at matched clean cost keep at most 40.0%, and this robustness comes from the global noise level: input-estimated masks and noise levels add none. These findings hold on a disjoint held-out set and on a second VLM and yield three requirements for defenses, we release the protocol.

摘要：

##### **Familiar Words, Unfamiliar Minds: Conventionalization without Calibration in the Vocabulary of Mind**
2611.34179v1 by Matthieu Queloz

Speakers describe new technologies by stretching old words, letting experience of the new referents sort out which inferences carry over. I argue that stretching the vocabulary of mind to language models pulls apart two processes that theories of metaphor and linguistic change tend to conflate: conventionalization, whereby a stretched word ceases to be marked as such, and calibration, whereby the inferences it invites are fitted to what its new referents bear out. As a gene variant can reach fixation without being an adaptation, a word can become conventional without being calibrated. A corpus study of "hallucinate" in 8,568 abstracts and 31,866 forum comments shows conventionalization running most of its course within three years. Calibration need not keep pace: these models are organized so unlike human minds that it is hard to tell when mental words apply and what they imply. Even warranted attributions can mislead: mechanistic analysis shows that a transformer trained to navigate Manhattan has and uses a faithful street map, yet loses track of its position on long, diverted journeys. Nor is the gap likely to close by itself. The usual routes to calibration (direct experience, designers' knowledge, experiment) presuppose a referent that is accessible, stable, and independent of the discourse about it. But language models are opaque, fast-changing, and looped into the discourse: they are trained on past uses of mental vocabulary and on the experimental literature about them, and join the discourse themselves. Calibration must therefore be organized, through a reciprocal, not merely deferential, division of linguistic labour. This yields a norm of targeted qualification (flag the specific inferences an attribution invites without support), a reply to the worry that meaning change eludes deliberate control, and three testable predictions about when qualifications calibrate expectations.

#### Abstracts
##### **$μ$-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-User Task Completion in Shared Interaction Voice Agents**
2611.34920v1 by Qihao Lin, Guanxu Chen, Shuhang Zheng, Xiaoya Lu, Jingyi Yang, Terumi Chiba, Wenyi Yu, Chao Zhang, Zhuan Shi, Jing Shao

Voice agents in shared spaces must satisfy interdependent multi-user requests while upholding per-user authority, privacy, and task constraints. Existing evaluations offer limited coverage when participants react throughout a session. We introduce $μ$-Bench, a benchmark for joint task completion in closed-loop, full-duplex multi-user voice interaction. Simulated participants act from isolated profiles of goals, knowledge, and permissions, adapting to the agent's speech and tool outcomes. Across 20 domains, 100 scenarios vary group size, acoustics, and follow-up demands to form 800 cases (408 tool-augmented, 392 tool-free). Joint Task Success (JTS) requires all user goals, privacy boundaries, and terminal states to hold jointly, while Pure JTS further disallows unauthorized tool calls; Attribution, Threading, and Coordination (ATC) diagnostics localize failures. Across 13 voice agents, proprietary systems peak at 62.24\% tool-free and 65.44\% tool-augmented success, while open-weight models remain below 20\%. ATC diagnostics show that open-weight models lag primarily in multi-turn attribution and threading, and controlled contrasts reveal that larger groups consistently degrade joint task success across capable systems. By coupling reactive multi-user simulation with verifiable session-level outcomes, $μ$-Bench provides a rigorous testbed for shared voice agents.

摘要：

##### **DRBench Pro: A Knowledge-Centric Benchmark for Professional Deep Research Agents**
2611.34852v1 by Han Huang, Yuchen Yan, Xingyu Wu, Yijun Li, Chao Deng, Jie Liu, Jin Ma, Yongliang Shen

Deep research agents gather information from multiple sources and synthesize it into reports on complex, open-ended questions. Existing benchmarks assess report quality along several dimensions, but lack a dedicated knowledge-centric evaluation of which research directions a report covers and how deeply it explores them. We introduce DRBench Pro, a benchmark that makes structured knowledge coverage the primary evaluation target. Our automated pipeline builds local knowledge trees from Wikipedia and uses sampled subgraphs to generate research queries and evidence-grounded rubrics. Each rubric corresponds to a relation in the tree, preserving the structure of the knowledge being evaluated. Agents receive only the research query. This structure allows us to measure breadth across major research directions and depth along related knowledge chains, with their geometric mean giving overall coverage. The benchmark includes 144 cases across 24 domains and 44,102 rubrics. Evaluating 16 deep research agents reveals different strengths in breadth and depth, with the best-performing agent reaching 57.04% overall coverage. Rubric judgments agree closely with human annotations, and agent rankings remain stable across the tested metric configurations.

摘要：

##### **Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent: Self-Structuring Learning through Question Answering**
2611.34841v1 by Masahiro Kato

We consider the problem of constructing knowledge bases for applications based on large language models, in which the structure of a knowledge base determines how an application can retrieve and connect the information needed to answer a question. We study Self-Structuring Learning, a setting in which questions and expected answers guide the construction of a persistent knowledge base whose editable structure can be a graph schema or the organization of linked knowledge documents. We propose Knowledge-Base Gradient Descent, which diagnoses discrepancies between expected answers and responses obtained through the current knowledge base. At each round, the method proposes a structural edit, rebuilds the knowledge base, and adopts the edit when the learning loss decreases. The retained structure is evaluated on other questions about the same sources. By randomizing over candidate edits, we express the loss change of an edit as a derivative with respect to the probability of selecting it, and we evaluate joint edits as single candidates because their changes interact. Schema Descent instantiates the method for graph schemas. In experiments on large documents, each a bundle of research papers, the updated knowledge cards and graph schemas obtain higher mean Answer F1 on held-out generated questions than their initial structures, with schema gains in every document. On human questions, mean F1 rises for schemas and falls for cards: the updated structures serve the kind of question they were learned from.

摘要：

##### **Repairing the Trustworthiness of Distilled Language Models**
2611.34831v1 by Kumar Shubham, Devendra Vyas, Vidushi Agarwal, Jayin Khanna, Prathosh AP

Knowledge distillation enables the deployment of large language models through smaller student models, but the compression process can introduce regressions in trustworthiness even when downstream performance is preserved. Repairing such failures is challenging because the original distillation corpus is often unavailable, while direct preference optimization may alter capabilities inherited from the teacher. We propose a targeted repair framework based on the proximal Bregman response function (PBRF) that addresses both limitations. We estimate the curvature required for PBRF using a surrogate proxy dataset, removing dependence on the original transfer corpus, and extend the framework to preference-based supervision through an odds-ratio objective. This enables the use of preferred and dispreferred response pairs while constraining changes to the distilled model. Our results show that the proposed approach improves targeted trustworthiness behaviors for both pre-training and post-training models while largely preserving downstream performance, providing a practical alternative to re-distillation or unconstrained preference optimization. Our experiment results show that across different distilled models, the trustworthy regression can be improved by up to 8% with less than 1% decrement in perplexity.

摘要：

##### **The Linguistic Knowledge Benchmark: Do Language Models Know the Foundations of Early-Grade Literacy?**
2611.34823v1 by Maxime Lelièvre, Alasdair Mackintosh, Dominic Andrews, Tobias Murray, Moritz Boos, Oliver G. B. Garrod

Many languages of instruction in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs) are poorly represented in the data used to train language models. Before these models can support early-grade literacy in such languages, we need to know whether they hold the linguistic knowledge of each language that literacy teaching relies on. To test this, we present the Linguistic Knowledge Benchmark and the reproducible, largely automated pipeline that turns curriculum documents, teacher guides and pupil books into a vetted question set organised on a fixed grid of nine linguistic domains and seven grade levels. The pipeline combines LLM-driven source survey, feature consolidation, item generation and quality control with an item-selection step inspired by item response theory and a final expert review. We instantiate it for Kiswahili (Kenya; 135 items) and English (United Kingdom; 137 items) and evaluate 121 and 119 models respectively. Frontier models exceed 90% accuracy in both languages, but accuracy falls steeply for smaller and cheaper models, and more so in Kiswahili. The two best models under 10 billion parameters reach 91.7% and 73.0% in English but only 51.9% and 40.7% in Kiswahili, and the mean accuracy of models deployable on mobile and tablet hardware is 47% in English against 24% in Kiswahili. The models most practical for low-resource classrooms are therefore the least capable in the languages they would serve. We release a 20-item sample per language, all pipeline prompts, open-source code, and a public leaderboard. This is the first rung of a three-level evaluation ladder for measuring and generating decodable text to support foundational literacy.

##### **TASK Memory: Beyond Text-Only Memory with Activation Steering and Knowledge Editing against Memory Hallucination**
2611.34762v1 by Jinkun Zhao, Wenjun Wu

LLM agents increasingly rely on external memory, yet they still hallucinate about what they remember. Often the correct memory is retrieved and placed in the prompt, but the answer contradicts it or the memory is applied to the wrong question. We call these use-time memory hallucinations. Across five open models, we find that they arise from the conflict between parametric knowledge and external memory and take three forms: memory-ignoring, where the model returns to a stale fact in its weights (up to 46% of queries); memory-misapplying, where it attributes the memory to unrelated entities (up to 24%, and 49% when retrieval is combined with weight editing); and version confusion, where 53% of answers about revised facts assert the superseded value when both versions remain stored. We propose TASK memory, which goes beyond text-only memory by combining a Text note with Activation Steering and Knowledge editing that share one in-model address. The text holds the content. A removable knowledge edit supplies the address through its subject key and supports multi-hop composition. A pair of steering vectors decides, query by query, whether the model adopts the memory or keeps its own answer; the vectors store no content and control how the text memory is used. On frozen models under a pre-registered, test-once protocol, TASK reduces memory-ignoring hallucinations to at most 1.5% in all ten model-dataset cells and cuts neighbour misuse from 39.2% to 7.5% (+10.8 pp composite on CounterFact). It improves a 300-memory library over BM25 RAG by 16.4 pp, an advantage that holds at 3,000 memories, resolves fact revisions without fact IDs, and adds 7.5 pp of multi-hop accuracy on MQuAKE. 21 of 23 cross-model comparisons (8B-27B, four families) are significant, and TASK beats three recent context-steering methods in 9 of 10 model-dataset cells.

##### **From Shared Patterns to Individual Evidence: Two-Stage Reasoning for Emerging Entities in Temporal Knowledge Graphs**
2611.34708v1 by Jinchuan Zhang, Lei Gao, Hongyu Jiang, Chong Mu, Chenang Cai, Ke Yan

Temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs) continually introduce emerging entities whose interaction histories evolve from zero-shot debuts into sparse, bursty few-shot trajectories. However, current paradigms are fragmented: pattern-transfer methods lack catalog-wide event memory, whereas online memory models suffer from endpoint-role asymmetry that degrades unseen-answer ranking. Furthermore, sparse early interactions exhibit both long-range dispersion and local redundancy under skewed distribution. To address these challenges, we propose INSPIRE (Integrating Shared Patterns and Individual Evidence for Reasoning), a two-stage forecasting framework over a known catalog. In Stage I, INSPIRE learns an availability-aware shared-pattern predictor that fuses event-updated memory across the full candidate catalog while applying coordinated query- and catalog-side masking. Subsequently, Stage II freezes this predictor and optimizes a lightweight individual-evidence adapter (EA) combining textual semantic priors with correlation-weighted long-range event statistics under support- and role-balanced co-answer supervision with prior-odds debiasing and soft residual alignment. In addition, we decompose evaluation by endpoint roles and track longitudinal emerging-answer cohorts via query-micro and answer-entity macro metrics. Across four benchmarks based on the ICEWS and GDELT event datasets, INSPIRE improves Emerging MRR by 16.73%-88.83% over the strongest baselines. Relative to paired Stage I predictors, EA further increases Emerging MRR by 8.28%-32.63%, Answer-new MRR by 7.81%-41.24%, and cohort macro MRR by 9.64%-39.94%, gains attributed to semantic priors and candidate-level score competition suppressing mismatched established competitors without zero-support prior shortcuts. The official code is publicly available at https://github.com/KcAcoZhang/INSPIRE.

##### **PoVi: Agentic Poetry-to-Video Generation with Knowledge Grounding and Figurative Understanding**
2611.34690v1 by Yuxia Zhu, Shiying Zhang, Lingzhuang Sun, Song Yang, Siheng Wu, Xinglong Xu, Sizhe Qiu, Hao Liang, Cheng Tan, Asher Qin, Wentao Zhang

Poetry-to-video generation requires more than translating text into visual scenes. Poetic language is highly compressed, often depends on historical and cultural knowledge, and frequently conveys meaning through metaphor, imagery, and other non-literal expressions. We introduce PoVi, a benchmark and agentic framework for grounded poetry-to-video generation, evaluating semantic grounding, discourse coherence, and audiovisual realization. PoVi reconstructs poem semantics into an evidence-bounded representation and progressively transforms them into narration, interpretation, character assets, and shot-level visual plans, followed by narration-aligned video generation. Across three planning backbones and four video generators, PoVi consistently improves over single-call generation, with the largest gains on implicit background knowledge and metaphorical relations. PoVi provides a testbed for studying knowledge-grounded, long-horizon multimodal generation beyond direct text-to-video synthesis.

##### **Evacuation Traffic Prediction for Dynamic Transportation Networks over Multiple Hurricanes: A Generalized Graph Learning Approach**
2611.34687v1 by Md Nafees Fuad Rafi, Samiul Hasan

High traffic demand during hurricane evacuations causes severe congestion on major highways, leading to delays and safety risks for evacuees. Predicting evacuation traffic in real-time can significantly enhance evacuation management efforts. However, evacuation traffic is highly dynamic and uncertain due to evolving hurricane characteristics and diverse population response. Each hurricane has unique characteristics and evacuation contexts, resulting in different spatial-temporal traffic patterns. Moreover, the structure of detector-based transportation networks varies across multiple hurricanes. Hence, a model trained on the data from one hurricane may not generalize well for others. This study proposes a generalized dynamic graph learning framework using traffic detectors and evacuation data from 12 historical hurricanes. Leveraging the dynamic nature of transportation network, we develop a Graph Convolutional Long Short-Term Memory (GCN-LSTM) model that predicts evacuation traffic up to 6 hours in advance. For an unseen hurricane (Milton, 2024), the model shows strong generalizability, achieving an RMSE of 280.6 and R2 of 0.96 for 1-hour prediction, and an RMSE of 459.8 and R2 of 0.89 for overall 6-hours prediction. We also introduce a modular training approach, where we evaluate performance improvements by sequentially incorporating data from multiple hurricanes. The proposed model outperforms state-of-the-art baseline models for overall 6-hour prediction horizons. Ablation experiments show an 8.4% improvement in RMSE for the proposed dynamic GCN-LSTM model compared to its static counterpart. Traffic management officials can use the developed model to understand future traffic conditions during hurricane evacuation in advance and take proactive measures to reduce delays during evacuation.

##### **Find the Clue, Not the Scene: Rethinking Reasoning for Entity Recognition in Vision-Language Models**
2611.34681v1 by Kyungmin Min, Soyeon Kim, Junseok Kim, Kyomin Jung

Answering a knowledge-based visual question requires first recognizing the entity in the image and then recalling knowledge about it. Thinking vision-language models (VLMs) reason for thousands of tokens before answering, yet it remains underexplored how well such thinking VLMs perform each of these two steps. We therefore analyze thinking VLMs on this task and make two key observations. (1) Recognition is the bottleneck, since 76.3% of errors across four models on ViQuAE stem from misrecognizing the entity, and 84.9% of these failures are resolved once the entity name is provided in the question. (2) Recognition is driven by entity-recall clues rather than by general descriptions of the image. An entity-recall clue is a distinctive visual detail that helps the model recall the entity, such as keys, a traditional attribute of Saint Peter. Based on these findings, we propose ClueRL, a reinforcement learning approach that extends Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with an advantage localized to the entity-recall clue in the thinking trace. Rather than injecting new entity knowledge, ClueRL changes how VLMs think: it leads them to attend more closely to visual details and spot these clues, helping them use the knowledge they already have. Consequently, it outperforms both GRPO and a perception-reward baseline on three entity recognition benchmarks. Moreover, this clue-seeking behavior generalizes beyond entity recognition, the only task used for training. Since recognition is the bottleneck, the gains carry over to knowledge-based visual question answering. They even extend to general multimodal reasoning, raising Qwen3.5-4B by 4.4 and 4.0 points on the ScienceQA and MMMU subsets of MMStar, respectively.

##### **RoboEvo$^2$: Recursive Robot Self-Improvement via Harness-Policy Co-Evolution**
2611.34675v1 by Chenzhi Liu, Jiehong Lin, Wei Huang, Xuzhao Huang, Haoyuan Li, Yang Zhang, Junchao Huang, Chenyeyang Cai, Bo Wang, Kazuhiro Kosuge, Zhongrui Wang, Xiaojuan Qi

Robots should adapt to unfamiliar environments and acquire new skills through interaction, then execute those skills efficiently with practice. Vision-language-action policies provide efficient control, but often need additional human demonstrations to handle unfamiliar settings; reasoning agents can discover solutions using broad prior knowledge and robot-control APIs, but repeated deliberation and repair are costly. Inspired by human learning through reasoning and practice, we propose RoboEvo$^2$, a framework for recursive harness-policy co-evolution. In Policy-to-Harness (P2H) evolution, policy failures guide a coding agent to search complementary Code, Code+ICL, and Code+Policy routes, consolidating verified solutions into reusable skills, routing strategies, and experience memory. In Harness-to-Policy (H2P) evolution, verified trajectories provide supervised training, while Counterfactual Preference Distillation (CPD) teaches the policy to favor successful corrections over failures from the same starting state. The improved policy provides stronger motor skills for subsequent search, while its remaining failures redirect harness evolution, closing the reciprocal loop. Through recursive co-evolution without additional human demonstrations, RoboEvo$^2$ improves standalone success from 11.0% to 84.1% and hybrid success from 30.4% to 90.5% on LIBERO-PRO, with similar gains and reduced agentic costs on RoboTwin C2R. Further experiments demonstrate autonomous task acquisition, cross-embodiment harness transfer, and 95.0% hybrid success on the Unitree G1-D. Our models and code will be publicly released.

##### **Do We Still Need Reinforcement Learning When We Can Read the World Model?**
2611.34656v1 by Mingchao Zhang, Miduo Cui, Kangze He, Yuanjun Li, Dapeng Li, Ce Zhang, Chuanchen Luo, Xue Yan, Mingzhe Xing, Zhumin Chen, Hangyu Mao, Bin Zhang, Zhiwei Xu

Reinforcement learning has achieved remarkable success in sequential decision-making, but learning effective policies often requires substantial interaction experience. Model-based reinforcement learning alleviates this dependence by leveraging world models, which are typically used to support planning or policy optimization. In this paper, we investigate an alternative paradigm in which autonomous LLM-based coding agents directly synthesize control policies by reasoning over readable world models. By examining environment source code, these agents translate environmental knowledge into executable decision rules and iteratively refine their policies using online feedback or offline data, without task-specific RL training. Experiments across diverse decision-making benchmarks show that synthesized policies match or outperform conventional RL methods while requiring substantially less experience. Further analyses reveal interpretable decision rules and how they evolve through feedback-driven refinement. Comparisons with policies generated without source code access and experiments under challenging conditions further demonstrate the benefits of readable world models. These findings suggest that direct policy synthesis can reduce reliance on trial and error, challenging the necessity of task-specific RL training.

##### **Procedure Inference as Memory: Learning Recruiter Decision Procedures from OKF-Structured Episodes in Multi-Session Hiring Assistants**
2611.34576v1 by Praveen Kumar Bodigutla, Yizhi Zhang, Sai Vivek Kanaparthy, Arup De

Multi-session hiring assistants must infer evolving recruiter preferences and decision patterns across projects and time. We introduce Open Knowledge Format (OKF)-indexed episodic memory to organize interactions and retrieve related events through their lineage. We also introduce Procedure Memory, which learns reusable decision procedures from these episodes through prompted reflection or reinforcement learning. We compare these approaches with no memory, full-history context, and conversation search, and evaluate Jev-class models for index navigation and GPT-5.6 Luna as a lower-cost alternative to GPT-5.4.
  In our evaluation, OKF-indexed retrieval achieves low-priority preference F1 scores of 0.93-0.95 at medium and high preference overlap between hiring projects. It significantly outperforms full-history context and conversation search in both settings, and no memory at high overlap, while using 69% fewer context tokens than full history. Procedure Memory reduces conversational turns by up to 39% relative to no memory. Qualitative and long-horizon evaluations provide evidence of temporal reasoning, with RL-learned procedures achieving 60% on both correct abstention and exact-field identification. Jev-class models improve conversational efficiency but more frequently miss temporally relevant evidence, highlighting the importance of evidence selection.

GPT-5.6 Luna performs comparably to GPT-5.4 at lower cost. These findings highlight the value of structured episodic memory and learned decision procedures for efficient personalization and temporal reasoning.

##### **AliceAI-Foundation-80B-A3B-Base**
2611.34529v1 by Nikolay Skachkov, Ekaterina Redina, Maksim Konoplev, Evgenii Uskov, Veronika Zykova, Ivan Tsarikov, Artemii Zakharov, Mariia Godunova, Dmitrii Lunin, Maria Nikiforova, Valerii Levanov, Anton Kalsin, Daniil Panteleev, Alexey Gerasev, Ilia Kopytin, Yermek Kapushev, Egor Liakhov, Irina Lialikova, Tatiana Taekina, Anastasia Volotova, Assol Kubaeva, Kirill Alekseev, Pavel Otlivanchik, Mukhammadfirdavs Kosimov, Vladislav Negodin, Ivan Vinogradov, Anton Andriushchenko, Maksim Nikitin, Daniil Sukhoi, Alexander Mazitov, Maksim Abraham, Philip Zmushko, Maksim Ignatov, Ivan Sapozhkov, Timofey Byzov, Edgar Shmavonian

We present AliceAI-Foundation-80B-A3B-Base, a mixture-of-experts language model with 80 billion total parameters and approximately 3 billion active parameters per token, trained from scratch at Yandex and released under the Apache 2.0 license. The model combines Kimi Delta Attention with full attention, uses Attention Residuals, and is trained with Muon. Training comprises general pretraining on 17.5 trillion tokens, two context-extension stages, and a dedicated stage for reasoning and agentic interaction. We describe the data collection and augmentation pipelines, evaluation protocols, architectural ablations, and scaling-law experiments. We identify the limitations of held-out training loss as a proxy for downstream performance and introduce an auxiliary loss on mixture-of-experts outputs to stabilize training at the predicted learning rate. Domain-specific data selection and augmentation improve factual knowledge, mathematics, and programming skills, while agent trajectories introduced during pretraining improve performance on agentic benchmarks after supervised fine-tuning. In zero-shot reasoning evaluation, the model achieves leading results among open base models of comparable size, with 88.7% pass@8 on IMO-AnswerBench and 82.9% pass@8 on LiveCodeBench v5-v6. To evaluate Russian-language factual knowledge, we introduce and release two benchmarks with their evaluation protocols: WikiWebFacts, which tests short factual questions drawn from encyclopedic sources and search queries, and HardMultiQA, which tests more demanding factual questions inspired by user interactions. Our model scores 86.5% on WikiWebFacts and 67.9% on HardMultiQA, compared with 83.2% and 65.4% for DeepSeek-V4-Flash-Base (284B total and 13B active parameters) and 72.8% and 54.5% for Nemotron-3-Super-120B-A12B-Base (120B total and 12B active parameters), outperforming both larger models on these benchmarks.

##### **Long-Horizon Planning with Latent World Models via Graph Anchors**
2611.34509v1 by Syed Nazmus Sakib, Abdul Monaf Chowdhury, Peizhen Li, Nafiul Haque, Chandak Chakma, Md Mehedi Hasan, Zehong Cao, Shifat E. Arman

Latent world models plan in a compact latent space by predicting the outcomes of actions. Their planners judge a plan by the latent distance between its
  predicted end state and the goal image. We show that this distance fails in two distinct ways. First, it loses directional signal a few steps from the
  goal, causing distant goals to fail. Second, it weighs the robot arm over the manipulated object, causing manipulation to fail at contact. We measure
  both failures directly on recorded data by asking whether a lower latent distance corresponds to true progress toward the goal. Across five
  environments, six visual representations (including raw pixels and V-JEPA 2), and nine Meta-World tasks, this ranking test cleanly separates the two
  failures, and the graph distance to a goal predicts where closed-loop planning breaks down. Both failures share a single remedy: replacing the distant
  goal with a nearby target that lies on a recorded trajectory. We realize this principle with Graph Anchors, a training-free planner for frozen latent
  world models. It builds a sparse graph once over the model's own training frames and aims each planning step at a node a few moves ahead along the
  shortest path to the goal, keeping paths on recorded transitions wherever contact matters. On goals up to 400 steps away, Graph Anchors raise LeWM's
  success from 13% to 85% on PointMaze-Medium, from 8% to 87% on PointMaze-Large, and from 5% to 71% on Push-T, where stronger search toward the goal
  fails and where prior training-free planners break down under missing step horizons, subsampling shortcuts, or fragmented contact.

##### **Evaluating Aviation Safety Knowledge Extraction with SmolLM2: From Zero-Shot to Retrieval-Grounded Prompting**
2611.34432v1 by Aziida Nanyonga, Mustafa Tahsin Yilmaz, Amir Hussain, Ugur Turhan, Graham Wild

Aviation accident narratives contain safety knowledge that is difficult to capture using structured occurrence fields alone. SmolLM2-1.7B-Instruct was evaluated under zero-shot, one-shot, fixed few-shot, and retrieval-grounded prompting using 300 National Transportation Safety Board narratives from 2000-2020, containing 3,763 reference concepts and 467 directed relations. No prompting strategy performed best across all dimensions. Compared with fixed few-shot prompting, retrieval improved verbatim grounding (59.45% vs. 54.02%) but reduced complete structural compliance (33.3% vs. 42.7%). Verbatim matching establishes source presence rather than evidential relevance. An exploratory comparison using 35 benchmark narratives showed that Qwen3-8B achieved higher concept micro-F1 across all prompting conditions and higher relation F1 under demonstration-based prompting, reaching 51.37% and 34.62%, respectively, under one-shot prompting. The small subset limits generalization. These findings support evaluating semantic correctness, source traceability, structural reliability, and directed relation accuracy separately.

##### **EvoLink-Bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Self-Evolving Agents in Long-Horizon Interdependent Task Streams**
2611.34400v1 by Wei Chen, Xiaodong Lu, Weihao Shen, Gongxun Li, Xingyu Guo, Qihang Sun, Fuwei Zhang, Zhao Zhang, Yikun Ban, Shuang Li, Deqing Wang, Fuzhen Zhuang

Large language model (LLM) agents are evolving from passive chatbots into autonomous systems that operate continuously in dynamic environments. Such agents need not only solve current tasks but also learn from past interactions to improve future performance. However, existing streaming benchmarks often rely on short task sequences or largely independent tasks, providing limited evidence of whether agents can effectively reuse experience when later tasks depend on earlier outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{EvoLink-Bench}, a benchmark for evaluating self-evolving agents in long-horizon, interdependent task streams. It covers daily office work, multi-turn dialogue, and AutoResearch-style workflows, with persistent workspaces and dependencies across tasks. The benchmark includes two complementary levels: \textbf{L1}, which evaluates the acquisition and revision of language-expressible knowledge, and \textbf{L2}, which assesses the development of task-level capabilities through execution and feedback. Its evaluation further distinguishes within-task recovery, persistent-state updates, and experience reuse in later tasks. We evaluate five agent frameworks across three backbone configurations under three persistence conditions, covering 19,635 scored task slots from selected evaluation cohorts. Results show that no framework consistently performs best across both levels, retries recover only a limited proportion of failed tasks, and persistent-state updates do not necessarily improve subsequent performance. Furthermore, the effects of evolution differ between knowledge- and capability-level tasks, suggesting that these two forms of evolution require different learning mechanisms. Performance gains also vary across domains and task streams, with improvements on some tasks not necessarily extending to others.

##### **Under the Influence -- of What? A Controlled Evaluation of Influence Functions**
2611.34373v1 by Johannes Gabriel Sindlinger, Arzu Burcu Güven, Rob van der Goot, Anna Rogers

Influence functions are a promising way to identify which training examples are responsible for a model's output. However, so far empirical evaluations on LLM pretraining level only assumed that they work as intended. This is the first work to systematically evaluate whether the 'influential' examples actually have properties relevant to a language model output, based on re-training with controlled data splits. We investigate three prominent Hessian approximation methods (GradSim, TrackStar, K-FAC) in two settings (linguistic phenomena and factual knowledge) in GPT-2 type models (68M-5.6B training tokens). We find that on average, the `influential' examples are indeed relevant, but they do not always bear the target properties directly, and the ratio and kind of relevance varies by setting.

##### **Learning from Unreliable Operator References: Claim-Level Rewards for Customer Support Agents**
2611.34341v1 by Dmitrii Stoianov, Olga Tsymboi, Danil Taranets, Daniil Strizhakov, Ilya Buryak, Almaz Dautov, Dmitry Fatnev, Arthur Babkin, Aleksandr Fida, Daniil Dryabin, Maksim Shilenko, Nikita Surnachev, Anatolii Potapov

Even a correct operator reply can be an unreliable training target for a customer-support agent. About a quarter of the factual claims in these replies lacked full support in the agent's knowledge base and tool records. We treat operator replies as claims to verify rather than answers to imitate. Unverified claims guide revisions to the knowledge base and tools. ##### **MeterRouter: Budget-Aware Dynamic Routing for Multi-Agent Systems**
2611.34327v1 by Yue Xu, Leikun Yin, Ruiming Min, Yufeng Lai, Justin Andrew Johnson, Sitao Luan, Xiaotong Shen

Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) outperform single-agent systems, but inter-agent communication incurs substantial token overhead. While assigning a large LLM to every agent is common, it results in inefficient resource utilization, leaving the system far from the Pareto frontier. The cost of answering a query is set jointly by how many agents are recruited, the role and LLM assigned to each, and communication topology among them. Most existing routers treat cost only implicitly rather than as an explicit optimization constraint. A fixed accuracy-cost preference also cannot express how much a caller is willing to pay for a particular query. We introduce MeterRouter, a budget-aware dynamic router that incrementally synthesizes a collaboration graph by selecting each agent's role, LLM, incoming connections, and termination conditioned on the query and a per-query monetary budget. We initialize the router by imitating successful execution graphs, then jointly refine its decisions with a variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) whose reward favors correct answers and penalizes spending and budget overruns. Across five benchmarks and a three-tier LLM pool, MeterRouter is (1) accurate, achieving performance comparable to the strongest MAS; (2) economical, reaching that accuracy at 82% lower cost than the strongest MAS router and extending the accuracy-cost frontier beyond the ceiling of single-agent systems; and (3) controllable, adapting its spending to the specified budget while rarely exceeding it.

##### **Crucible: Evaluating LLM Agents in Executable Chemistry Research Environments**
2611.34254v1 by Yinjun Liu, Xinye Li, Ruisen Peng, Lang Gao, Zixiang Xu, Zirui Song, Xiuying Chen

Large language models (LLMs) perform well on chemistry knowledge benchmarks, which mainly assess question answering and isolated tasks. Chemical research, however, requires using specialized tools and interpreting their outputs to obtain chemically valid results. To address this gap, we introduce Crucible Bench to evaluate this ability across all four stages of the design-make-test-analyze (DMTA) cycle. The benchmark contains 1,078 tasks in 12 task families curated from 18 published datasets, covering molecular design, synthesis planning, experimental execution, closed-loop optimization, simulation, and analytical characterization. Agents access 75 domain-specific tools through the Model Context Protocol in seven isolated environments. Sandboxed graders verify the resulting synthesis routes, experimental protocols, and other outputs. Laboratory tasks use robotic simulators or previously measured experimental data. We evaluate 14 LLMs as agents and find that the best achieves an overall score of 0.545, with synthesis planning and closed-loop optimization emerging as the hardest domains. Overall performance does not predict optimization performance well: the model ranked twelfth overall achieves the highest score in closed-loop optimization. Error analysis also shows that agents often submit chemically invalid results despite running their tools successfully. Stronger models benefit more from tool access, but reliable use of chemical software remains a challenge that Crucible Bench can track as models improve.

##### **MedSkill-E$^{2}$: Evaluating and Evolving Skills for Medical Agents**
2611.34250v1 by Jingjing Hua, Shaocong Wei, Jianghan Shen, Junzhi Ning, Min Cen, Cheng Tang, Ziyan Huang, Jiepeng Li, Fengshuo Liu, Kangkun Mao, Mouxiao Bian, Yirong Chen, Ming Hu, Jiyao Liu

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills to access domain knowledge and complete real-world tasks. We observe that even when a skill specifies the rules and procedures needed for a task, agents may fail to apply them effectively during autonomous execution, a phenomenon we call skill Enable But Underuse (EBU). This issue is particularly consequential in medical tasks, where overlooking a single critical constraint can yield a plausible but incorrect result. To evaluate and address EBU, we introduce MedSkill-E$^{2}$, a framework for evaluating and evolving skills for medical agents. We construct EBU-Bench, a challenging benchmark covering 12 medically relevant task groups and associated skills, to evaluate agents' ability to use available skills autonomously. We quantify the EBU gap as the difference in skill-use accuracy between autonomous use and explicitly guided reading and application of the same skill. On EBU-Bench, all evaluated model-harness combinations exhibit EBU, with a mean gap of 43.94 percentage points (pp). For the same model, skill-use accuracy under autonomous use differs by up to 38.96 pp across harnesses. Motivated by these observations, we propose EBU-Evolve, which uses execution feedback from autonomous and explicitly guided skill use to distill effective guidance into reusable skill instructions. Relative to their pre-evolution versions, the revised skills improve skill-use accuracy during autonomous execution by 33.33 to 55.17 pp and continue to yield improvements when transferred across multiple models and harnesses. Our results show that autonomous skill-use accuracy varies across harnesses with the model and skill held fixed, and model rankings can change. EBU can persist even after a skill is opened. EBU-Evolve improves autonomous skill-use accuracy on unseen tasks within the same skill, with gains transferring across models and harnesses.

摘要：

##### **Did the Model Forecast It, or Already Know It? Evaluating Large Language Models for Novel Drug-Target Prioritisation with a Temporal Knowledge-Graph Oracle**
2611.34247v1 by Pui Chung Siu, Yuan Liang, Claudia Cabrera, Mani Mudaliar, Arkaitz Zubiaga

AI systems increasingly help decide which drug targets enter costly clinical trials. We recast each target-disease hypothesis as a dated event, the pair's first clinical trial. This lets us test whether a language model can forecast such events after its training cutoff, rather than recall events before it. We rebuild a dated biomedical knowledge graph as it stood at the end of each year from 2020 up to its newest version. We then ask six open language models, with and without this graph, which targets will enter a first trial for a disease the next year. Without retrieval, the models rank about as well as trial history, a count of each target's past trials in the same therapeutic area, also after their training cutoffs. Evidence dated before the decision adds at most a small gain, before or after the models' training cutoffs. The newest graph raises five of six models clearly above that baseline, but its paths usually include the answer: the pair's own trial, dated after the decision. Evaluations of target prioritisation should date both features and labels, with evidence from before the decision and outcomes after it.

##### **Familiar Words, Unfamiliar Minds: Conventionalization without Calibration in the Vocabulary of Mind**
2611.34179v1 by Matthieu Queloz

Speakers describe new technologies by stretching old words, letting experience of the new referents sort out which inferences carry over. I argue that stretching the vocabulary of mind to language models pulls apart two processes that theories of metaphor and linguistic change tend to conflate: conventionalization, whereby a stretched word ceases to be marked as such, and calibration, whereby the inferences it invites are fitted to what its new referents bear out. As a gene variant can reach fixation without being an adaptation, a word can become conventional without being calibrated. A corpus study of "hallucinate" in 8,568 abstracts and 31,866 forum comments shows conventionalization running most of its course within three years. Calibration need not keep pace: these models are organized so unlike human minds that it is hard to tell when mental words apply and what they imply. Even warranted attributions can mislead: mechanistic analysis shows that a transformer trained to navigate Manhattan has and uses a faithful street map, yet loses track of its position on long, diverted journeys. Nor is the gap likely to close by itself. The usual routes to calibration (direct experience, designers' knowledge, experiment) presuppose a referent that is accessible, stable, and independent of the discourse about it. But language models are opaque, fast-changing, and looped into the discourse: they are trained on past uses of mental vocabulary and on the experimental literature about them, and join the discourse themselves. Calibration must therefore be organized, through a reciprocal, not merely deferential, division of linguistic labour. This yields a norm of targeted qualification (flag the specific inferences an attribution invites without support), a reply to the worry that meaning change eludes deliberate control, and three testable predictions about when qualifications calibrate expectations.

##### **Will AI Replace Juniors? How Task Complexity Reduces Automation Risk**
2611.34099v1 by Vincent Rost, Fabian Stephany

How does task complexity shape firms' willingness to substitute entry-level labour with artificial intelligence (AI) tools? We study this question using a pre-registered survey experiment with managers from 1,435 German firms in the information economy and manufacturing industry. Managers are randomly assigned to consider a long lasting need for additional capacity involving either well-structured and standardised tasks, or more complex tasks that require professional judgement, contextual knowledge and independent problem-solving. Relative to standardised tasks, complex tasks reduce willingness to use AI rather than hire an entry-level employee by 0.54 points on a five-point scale and increase the importance attached to human oversight by 0.15 points. Further, pre-treatment responses indicate that managers expect the availability of AI applications to reduce hiring of both entry-level employees and experienced workers, but significantly more strongly for entry-level workers.

##### **Too Smart for Its Own Good? Cognitive Fidelity and Competence Leakage in LLM Simulations of Young Learners**
2611.34042v1 by Zhen Xu, Beata Beigman Klebanov, Jamie N. Mikeska

LLM-based interactive learner simulators are increasingly used in educational applications, including teacher training and tutoring system development. A critical requirement for those simulations is cognitive fidelity: the simulated learner should maintain the assigned knowledge, misconceptions, and reasoning procedures throughout interaction. However, LLMs may express knowledge or problem-solving skills beyond the assigned learner's cognitive status, a phenomenon we refer to as competence leakage. In this exploratory study, we examine 3,339 simulated learner utterances from 130 teacher-learner simulator transcripts across three learning scenarios and identify over-competence as a prominent direction of cognitive-profile misalignment during interaction. We then investigate the recurrence of previously observed competence leakage cases under four matched-trigger evaluation protocols, ranging from isolated questioning to multi-turn interactions, across five model configurations. Overall, we observe higher violation recurrence under multi-turn protocols than under isolated question answering. More capable models generally show lower recurrence, whereas increased reasoning effort does not consistently improve cognitive fidelity and is even associated with slightly higher recurrence under multi-turn protocols in our matched-model comparison. These findings provide exploratory evidence of competence leakage phenomena in interactive learner simulation and suggest that single-turn question-answering type of evaluations alone may be insufficient, motivating the development of more systematic and reliable multi-turn protocols for evaluating cognitive fidelity in learner simulation.

##### **Incorporating the Knowledge Distillation to Improve the EfficientNet Transfer Learning Capability**
2611.34025v1 by Tjeng Wawan Cenggoro

Due to the Deep Learning requirement for a large training dataset, Transfer Learning has become a central method in the field of Computer Vision, which heavily used Deep Learning. Since the adoption of transfer learning in the field, the performance of the models in computer vision is significantly upgraded. The common transfer learning practice in computer vision research is to use state-of-the-art architectures in the ImageNet dataset as the backbone network. The best performing architecture in ImageNet is currently held by EfficientNet, which is a set of architectures that were progressively grown from a baseline architecture.
  By observing the EfficientNet development approach, it is natural to hypothesize that the smaller EfficientNet architecture is capable to contain the knowledge of the larger architecture. This hypothesis comes from the fact that the larger EfficientNet architecture was grown from the smaller architecture. Therefore, in this study, we experimented if it is beneficial to transfer the knowledge from the larger architecture to the smaller architecture of the EfficientNet. To achieve this goal, we proposed a transfer learning method that uses Knowledge Distillation as the knowledge transfer mechanism from the larger to smaller architecture. We found that the proposed method is able to upgrade the performance of each of the EfficientNet architecture. Several smaller architectures are even able to outperform the larger architecture that was trained using the standard transfer learning method.

##### **Assessing the Physical Interpretability of AlphaEarth Foundation Embeddings Through Multi-Sensor Satellite Time-Series Benchmarking for Agricultural Mapping**
2611.34019v1 by El Houcine El Moussaoui, Chouaib El Hachimi, Saïd Khabba, Salwa Belaqziz, Bouchra Ait Hssaine, Abderrahman Lahrouni, Salah Er-Raki, Abdelghani Chehbouni

Accurate and dynamic maps of agricultural LULC are important for agricultural water resource management, particularly in areas where water scarcity is prevalent, such as Morocco. Here, we evaluate the utility of AlphaEarth Foundation Model (AEF) embeddings for agricultural LULC and irrigation-type mapping in Morocco. AEF embeddings were compared to single-date and time-series remote-sensing features using three machine-learning classifiers: XGBoost, CatBoost, and TabPFN. We also evaluated whether AEF embeddings can: (a) provide an efficient and transferable alternative to conventional direct use of Earth observation products and (b) reconstruct biophysical information. Results showed that multi-temporal feature time series achieved the highest performance, with accuracies of 0.93 for agricultural LULC and 0.87 for irrigation-type mapping when using TabPFN. However, AEF embeddings yielded comparable results, achieving accuracies of 0.91 for agricultural LULC and 0.85 for irrigation-type mapping, while reducing the need for extensive data processing and manual feature engineering typically required for satellite products. Spatial LULC mapping using TabPFN achieved comparable overall accuracies (0.85-0.86) across the three feature configurations, but revealed substantial differences in mapped tree cover, ranging from 14.57% for surface reflectance to 29.79% for AFE.
