Future research should focus on enhancing the interpretability of embedding-based approaches by incorporating physical knowledge to develop more transparent and trustworthy AI systems for agricultural applications.

##### **AI Buckingham: Agentic Discovery of Dimensionless Scaling Laws Across Physical Regimes**
2611.33961v1 by Ao Cheng, Lei Zhang, Guowei He

Classical dimensional analysis defines admissible dimensionless representations but does not determine which combinations capture the dominant physical dependence, whether it changes across physical regimes, or when dependent-variable rescaling is required. We present AI Buckingham, an agentic framework for autonomous dimensionless discovery from natural-language descriptions of physical problems and observational data. It couples reasoning and planning by a large language model (LLM) with predefined symbolic and numerical tools. Literature-based knowledge retrieval supports dimensional checking, gradient-based clustering identifies physical regimes, and mutual information provides an objective, reparameterization-invariant criterion for identifying dominant dimensionless groups. Explicit numerical criteria determine the group count and assess simplification. Across ten physical systems spanning fluid dynamics, magnetohydrodynamics, thermal convection and additive manufacturing, AI Buckingham recovers established similarity representations and resolves regime-dependent scalings. For the longstanding challenge of predicting turbulent drag on rough surfaces, it identifies informative reparameterizations of surface statistics that improve equivalent sand-grain roughness estimates for engineering-oriented drag modelling.

##### **Contextual language representations link narrative processing to language and literacy in children**
2611.33871v1 by Dongyi He, Tian Jiang, Nizhuan Wang, Wai Ting Siok

Spoken narratives extensively connect moment-to-moment language processing with knowledge accumulated through language and literacy experience. This study examined that relationship using functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) during Cantonese story listening and contextual representations from large language model (LLM). The imaging cohort comprised 23 school-age children; 16 contributed complete standardized literacy and language assessments and served as held-out participants in a nested cross-subject encoding analysis of the same story. Contextual embeddings predicted distributed narrative responses, with prominent correspondence in temporoparietal and default-network parcels. Regional summaries of the top 25% of voxels ranked by held-out correlation yielded atlas-wide means of r=0.174 and partial R2=0.0165. A joint feature model identified negative surprisal and positive predictive-entropy coefficients across cortical and subcortical parcels, including control, salience, hippocampal and amygdalar regions. Higher word-dictation and overall literacy scores were associated with lower regional encoding performance. Higher Cantonese grammar scores were associated with more negative semantic-update coefficients in parietal control, somatomotor and dorsal-attention parcels. Together, these findings connect language and literacy skills to the regional strength and direction of neural coupling with narrative context. They position naturalistic context processing as a possibly measurable link between oral comprehension and literacy in childhood.

##### **ChemDeduce: Benchmarking LLMs for Unknown Chemical Identification through Interactive Simulated Experiments**
2611.33848v1 by Hengyi Cai, Yuqi Tang, Zichang Li, Huajun chen, Qiang Zhang, Keyan Ding

Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance in chemical knowledge and reasoning, yet it remains unclear whether they can solve problems whose answers must be discovered through experimentation. We investigate this question through Unknown Chemical Identification, where a model must actively design experiments, interpret observations, update hypotheses, and ultimately identify a hidden substance. We introduce ChemDeduce, an interactive evaluation framework for assessing LLMs through simulated experiments. ChemDeduce formulates chemical identification as a sequential decision process and provides a stateful simulated laboratory with explicit instrument and operational constraints, enabling scalable and reproducible evaluation of iterative experimental reasoning. We benchmark eight frontier LLMs on 200 chemicals, yielding 1600 model--target pairs and 4800 episodes over three independent repetitions, and evaluate identification accuracy, operational compliance, interaction efficiency, and trajectory-level reasoning quality. Despite generating seemingly plausible experimental plans, current models achieve an average identification accuracy ranging from 23.83% to 67.33%, revealing substantial limitations in experiment selection, evidence interpretation, and sequential hypothesis refinement.

##### **MetaDecomp: Adaptive Task Decomposition via Meta-Learned Procedural Memory**
2611.33822v1 by Ziyi Wang, Yixiao He, Yichen Wu, Ruite Lu, Jiayu Wang, Ying Wei

Reusing past experience in long-horizon tasks requires more than storing successful procedures: Language agents must distinguish which procedures apply and what work remains as execution progresses. We introduce MetaDecomp, an in-context meta-learning framework for adaptive task decomposition that jointly learns a task-relative state space and reusable procedures. Its outer loop builds procedural memory from successful training executions, representing task progress as abstract states and procedures between states as directed acyclic graphs of operations and dependencies. As new progress distinctions are learned, MetaDecomp revisits historical execution evidence to reassess state assignments and reorganize the procedures they support. With memory fixed at test time, the inner loop refines an initial decomposition and revises it from environmental feedback, combining applicable procedure fragments with task-specific steps missing from memory. Evaluated on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld over three seeds, MetaDecomp achieves the highest mean performance among the compared methods in four of five settings, for example, an 86.3% success rate on ALFWorld with Qwen3-4B. Fixing the initial schema or omitting historical reprojection reduces performance in component ablations. MetaDecomp also uses fewer inference tokens than ReAct and AutoRefine in all five settings. Cross-benchmark analysis identifies a low-performance area where partial coverage degrades success relative to hidden memory; high coverage improves success, while low coverage changes little. Code will be released upon acceptance.

##### **QPAT: A Universal Quantum Machine Learning Framework for Quantum-Assisted Training and Classical Inference**
2611.33806v1 by Yixiong Chen

Deep learning models often struggle with sharp local optima and overfitting in limited-data regimes, and exhibit limited capacity for capturing high-order, complex probability distributions. Quantum Machine Learning (QML) models, by operating in exponentially large Hilbert spaces, can efficiently represent such distributions and provide favorable inductive biases for learning, offering a promising pathway to overcome these limitations. However, their practical deployment in the Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era is severely hindered by gradient coupling, barren plateaus, data-loading bottlenecks, and high inference latency. To bridge classical AI and QML, we propose QPAT (QCBM-Prior-Assisted Training), a universal framework that employs a Quantum Circuit Born Machine (QCBM) as a learnable generative prior to provide a task-aligned structured inductive bias during classical model training. The framework can be seamlessly integrated into any classical backbone, including LSTMs, CNNs, and Transformers. We introduce a decoupled fitness-based scoring mechanism and a stochastic "Drop-Prior" mechanism to implicitly distill quantum structural knowledge into the classical weights during training. Evaluated extensively on six benchmarks spanning time-series forecasting, computer vision, and natural language processing, our framework consistently outperforms purely classical backbones in both predictive performance and stability across random seeds; ablation studies further show that these gains are not reproduced by unstructured random priors or a classically learned generative prior. Remarkably, our approach enables completely classical inference, bypassing quantum latency while preserving quantum-enhanced accuracy, paving a highly practical path for QML deployment.

##### **Teaching Students to Stand on Their Own: Distilling Vision Foundation Models for Fine-Grained Few-Shot Class-Incremental Learning**
2611.33749v1 by Junsu Kim, Seungryul Baek

Fine-grained few-shot class-incremental learning (FG-FSCIL) aims to incrementally learn visually similar new classes from limited images without catastrophic forgetting. Prior work typically learns strong representations on base classes or leverages vision foundation models (VFMs) directly as backbones or via knowledge distillation. However, VFM backbones incur high computational costs and do not guarantee fine-grained recognition. Although knowledge distillation reduces inference cost, our preliminary experiments reveal that distilled students still lag substantially behind their VFM teachers, particularly on new classes. To address this limitation, we propose STAND, a systematic VFM-to-student distillation design tailored for FG-FSCIL. Specifically, STAND connects the teacher and student by equipping the student with a fixed copy of the teacher's classifier, aligning their outputs via projectors, and feeding both models composite images that combine different training images. On these composite images, relation distillation matches how the teacher and the student relate each image to other images, while difference distillation matches how this relation changes between two composite images. Finally, we freeze the student and classify new classes using length-calibrated prototypes, which restore the reduced similarity of few-shot prototypes. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that STAND achieves state-of-the-art performance across FG-FSCIL datasets at low computational cost, while generalizing across student backbones and broader datasets.

##### **REFORGE: LLM-Assisted RTL Bug Repair with Structured Context and Verification Feedback**
2611.33748v1 by Tanvir Hossain, Sudipta Paria, Aidan Gleason Myers, Swarup Bhunia, Tamzidul Hoque

Large language models (LLMs) can write and repair RTL, but successful compilation or testbench execution does not establish defect removal, behavioral preservation, security, or implementation feasibility. Existing LLM-based RTL repair methods often report a single repair rate without retaining separate evidence for these properties, limiting reproducible comparison and integration into hardware design flows. We present REFORGE, which, to our knowledge, is the first LLM-assisted RTL repair framework that maintains and accumulates structured repair evidence across iterations, allowing it to generate fixes that satisfy multiple objectives simultaneously, including functionality, behavioral equivalence, security, and cost efficiency. REFORGE profiles buggy RTL, identifies structural and security-relevant features, localizes failing behavior, and uses verification-guided diagnosis to generate complete RTL repair variants. Each variant undergoes syntax checking, simulation, formal analysis, off-trigger preservation, security re-scan, and synthesis-based power, performance, and area analysis. We implement REFORGE as a backend-independent repair-and-verification flow and evaluate it in 288 runs across three LLM backends and three benchmark suites. On CirFix, the proposed context and diagnosis increase runs delivering a checked repair from 32/63 to 51/63, while every configuration repairs the Hack@DAC OpenTitan defect. Evidence records also show that a testbench-only criterion reports 3.9 times more repairs than the full set of applicable checks, security-clean outputs may lack functional verification, and different RTL repairs can implement equivalent hardware. These results show that LLM-generated RTL repairs require explicit multi-stage verification evidence rather than a single pass/fail outcome.

2611.33747v1 by Xian Cui, Gan Yang, Dongqi Han, Han Zhu, Bo Zheng, Jianhui Zheng, Xiaoyong Li

##### **WhatIf: Counterfactual World Models over Action-Grounded Causal Graphs for Robotic Manipulation**
2611.33745v1 by Xu Yang, Kailai Sun, Qianchuan Zhao

World models predict what happens under an action, but choosing between actions requires comparing their outcomes in the same physical situation. Factual transitions alone determine action-wise marginal futures and average effects, but not individual effects or the probability that switching actions causes harm. We introduce WhatIf, a counterfactual world model for manipulation that couples candidate futures through shared latent physics and transition noise inferred from factual history. Each candidate action induces a sparse object-factor causal graph describing the physical pathways it activates. Rolling out these graphs under the shared situation anchors predictions to observed outcomes and confines action contrasts to the objects each action reaches. Shared type-conditioned functions support varying numbers of objects, while a round-trip loss enforces action-invariant abduction without counterfactual labels. We show that replacing an individual effect with its conditional mean incurs mean-squared error equal to its conditional variance, motivating comparisons between coupled futures. Building on these comparisons, Counterfactual Advantage Planning (CAP) ranks plans by their advantage over an incumbent within shared situations and constrains the probability of harm. Paired advantages reduce estimator variance by a factor of $1-ρ$, where $ρ$ is the correlation between branch outcomes, and cancel model errors shared by both branches. Each executed transition provides hindsight advantages to update the physics posterior, the next plan, and an amortized policy without a learned critic. We further establish when observational transitions identify interventional dynamics. Paired simulator interventions evaluate counterfactual accuracy, graph recovery, compositional generalization, and decision quality.

##### **FlashProve-Next: Verifiable Inference for Production LLM APIs**
2611.33741v1 by Liang Song

Clients of closed-weight LLM APIs cannot verify whether a provider ran the advertised model, precision, hidden prompt, and reasoning budget.   FlashProve-Next produces verifiable, zero-knowledge-masked proofs for individual LLM API responses without modifying native serving (bf16/fp8/mxfp4, random sampling, FlashAttention, CUDA graphs, batching, prefix caching) and without disclosing weights, hidden prompts, hidden reasoning, or activations (dense profiles fix coarse geometry; cross-architecture hiding is deferred). Cost-weighted spot-checking supports non-interactive public verification (NI-315, $\varepsilon \le 2^{-40}$, $Q \le 2^{64}$) and external-seed C_pre mode (ExtSeed-153, $\varepsilon \le 2^{-20}$, $Q_{\rm samp} \le 2^{30}$).

##### **Rethinking Sequential Locate-Then-Edit: Optimality and Stability**
2611.33692v1 by Bingqing Liu, Wei Liu, Zhiying Deng, Jun Wang, Yuhua Li, Ruixuan Li

The Locate-Then-Edit framework enables efficient knowledge updates in large language models without requiring full retraining. Recent research has increasingly focused on sequential editing, where knowledge arrives continuously and the model is updated incrementally. However, existing sequential methods commonly suffer from catastrophic forgetting and model collapse after only a few hundred edits. In contrast, batch editing - which jointly optimizes all edits and yields the optimal solution under a specified editing objective - can support tens of thousands of edits with high edit efficacy while preserving the model's general capabilities, resulting in a substantial performance gap between the two editing paradigms. In this paper, we demonstrate that this gap can be closed via a batch-equivalent sequential editing approach. Moreover, we investigate the underlying mechanism of model degradation in existing sequential methods by analyzing the convergence behavior of weight perturbations as edits accumulate. We develop an automated framework for constructing test cases and use it to create 100 cases across 15 problem families, each formulated within this prover-verifier framework. Experiments with 10 frontier LLMs across multiple agent harnesses show that reliably producing verifiable proofs in accordance with specified protocols remains a substantial challenge. Our case studies further provide actionable insights into improving agents' ability to generate verifier-accepted proofs: tool use is essential for completing practical proof tasks; different forms of guidance, ranging from engineering instructions to problem-specific knowledge, substantially affect success rates on interactive-proof tasks; and proof-solving experience distilled from abstract tasks can transfer to concrete, real-world verification tasks.

However, in long-horizon manipulation, sparse success/failure feedback provides little information about where policies encounter bottlenecks. Moreover, repeated full-task rollouts also leave these bottlenecks weakly analyzed and insufficiently practiced. To this end, we introduce ReFocus, a progress-guided multi-round RL framework that alternates between Global and Local RL to identify task-specific bottlenecks and allocate additional practice to them. Specifically, during Global RL, we decompose each task into a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) of subtasks. Then a DAG-based Progress Reward is leveraged for both policy training and bottleneck diagnosis. Based on the identified bottlenecks, ReFocus selects Adaptive Practice Windows, defining task segments from an entry stage to a target stage. It allocates additional interaction to these segments during Local RL while updating the entire policy. After that, it evaluates the policy to perform the full task for the next round of RL, forming an iteration between Global and Local RL. Experiments on LIBERO-Long and RoboCasa365 show that ReFocus achieves success rates of 97.8% and 51.3%, respectively, compared with 90.6% and 39.3% for the $π_{0.5}$ baseline. Furthermore, we evaluate a real-world adaptation on three long-horizon tasks. On BreadBaker, ReFocus achieves 95% success, compared with 30% for the $π_{0.5}$. Project page: https://refocus-robotics.github.io.

##### **Privacy Disclosure Control Benchmark (PDC-Bench): How Faithfully Do LLMs Keep Privacy Constraints on In-Context Disclosure Granularity?**
2611.32610v1 by Kosuke Fujita, Daiki Nishiyama, Jun Sakuma

In LLM task execution, a constraint on the permitted disclosure granularity of personal attributes is sometimes imposed on the output. For example, if a person's private attribute is their location (San Francisco) and disclosure is permitted only at the country level, an output at the state level (California) violates the constraint. Detecting such violations requires capturing not only direct privacy leakage (San Francisco) but also semantic leakage (California). However, existing benchmarks cannot measure the degree of leakage with respect to a given semantic constraint such as the country-level limit. We therefore formalize disclosure control as a constraint on the permitted ceiling over the hierarchy of a knowledge graph. Building on this formulation, we construct PDC-Bench, a benchmark of 16,105 samples covering geography, age, race and ethnicity, and occupation. In our experiments applying PDC-Bench to eleven LLMs, 62% of the constraint violations were semantic leakage. We further found that semantic leakage depends less on the model than on the permitted disclosure granularity and the task's need for the private attribute; reliable auditing must therefore target semantic leakage together with these two factors. Data, prompts, and code are available at https://github.com/fuji1655/PDC-Bench.

##### **Evaluating Latent Knowledge Stability in Multimodal Large Language Models**
2611.32566v1 by Chuancheng Shi, Shangze Li, Wenhua Wu, Zifeng Cheng, Pengyang Shao, Fei Shen, Tat-Seng Chua

Changes in image viewpoint and question formulation can alter latent knowledge representations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, output correctness and overall representational drift provide limited insight into how these changes affect model decisions. We therefore evaluate latent knowledge stability across visual and linguistic elicitation conditions from two complementary perspectives: answer preference and internal support. Specifically, we devise the margin retention score (MRS), which measures the first-order projected degradation of the initial answer's logit margin over competing options across conditions and assesses how changes in latent knowledge representations affect this margin. We also present latent knowledge support-structure stability (KSS), which evaluates reference-correct items by comparing internal support profiles constructed using the margin gradient under the reference condition, which jointly reflects changes in how internal support is distributed and changes in total support magnitude. We further construct MMStableBench, comprising 19,452 knowledge items (facts), each probing the same latent knowledge across varied image viewpoints, semantically equivalent question formulations, and answer-option permutations. Experiments across 15 MLLMs show that MRS and KSS reveal latent knowledge instability not captured by accuracy, while an MRS-inspired regularizer further improves the generalization of edited latent knowledge.

##### **Progressive Reliable Supervision for Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation**
2611.32474v1 by Jing Li, Zhe Zhang, Huateng Sun, Yuhui Yang, Wanli Xue, Xu Cheng, Fan Shi, Jianhua Zhang, Qinghua Hu, Shengyong Chen

Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation (B$^2$-UniDA) aims to adapt a target model to an unlabeled target domain under arbitrary label-space relationships, while neither source data nor source-model parameters are accessible during adaptation. Under such restrictive information access, reliable target supervision is unavailable, while predictions from the black-box source model can be unreliable due to source bias and domain shifts. To address this challenge, we propose Progressive Reliable Supervision (PReS), which progressively establishes and refines reliable target supervision by coordinating complementary knowledge from the black-box source model, foundation models, and the evolving target model. PReS first identifies reliable known samples by exploiting semantic consistency under diffusion-based perturbations and cross-model consensus between the black-box source model and CLIP. It then estimates the sample-wise reliability of the two predictors and adaptively integrates their complementary predictions to construct reliable pseudo labels. The resulting supervision is exploited asymmetrically to encourage confident and discriminative predictions for reliable known samples while maintaining uncertain yet locally consistent predictions for potential unknown samples. As adaptation proceeds, the emerging target-domain knowledge is further leveraged to refine both the pseudo labels and the known/unknown sample partition, forming a closed-loop process in which supervision construction and target adaptation mutually reinforce each other. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets under diverse label-space relationships demonstrate that PReS achieves state-of-the-art or highly competitive performance under the restrictive B$^2$-UniDA setting.

##### **ASTERISM: Do Multimodal LLMs Actually Look at All the Images?**
2611.32316v2 by Chanseul Cho, Dongwoo Kim, Noori Bae, Seungjae Moon, Seongsu Lee, Youngmin Ro

Multi-image benchmarks aim to test whether a multimodal large language model (MLLM) combines evidence across images, but many of their items can be answered from a single image. Across about 33k items from 19 public benchmarks, our audit and a two-stage single-image filter find that one image or the question alone settles 24.2% of them. On these items, MLLMs recover from one image a median 78% of the accuracy that all images add over the text alone, so these items reward answering from one image, which we call the single-image shortcut. MLLMs take this shortcut even on items where one image is not enough. On such items, most of their wrong answers repeat an answer they give from one image, at a rate well above chance. We build ASTERISM, to our knowledge the largest audited multi-image benchmark, from these 19 benchmarks by keeping their items that need more than one image. Its 18.7k items, 2.0k of them corrected or rewritten, share one format, one scorer and seven task categories. We also release ASTERISM-Train with 49k training items and ASTERISM-Lite, a hard subset whose items we manually verify one by one. On ASTERISM-Lite, the strongest proprietary MLLM scores 68%. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), now common in MLLM post-training, rewards the shortcut as well, because a correct answer read off one image earns as much reward as one that combines images. In line with this, existing RLVR objectives add little to the ASTERISM score. To stop rewarding the shortcut in training as ASTERISM does in evaluation, we propose cross-image credit assignment (CICA), an extension of RLVR that moves the credit toward the tokens that need more than one image. On three open-source MLLMs, CICA raises the ASTERISM score by 2.6 to 6.0 points, more than twice the gain of each general and vision-specific RL objective trained on the same data.

##### **ExpGuard: Guarding Long-Horizon LLM Agents against Corrupted Experience**
2611.32085v1 by Yuzhou Chen, Licheng Pan, Zhixuan Chu, Yunsheng Lu, Yichen Sun, Han Zheng, Yan Wang, Bingyu Zhu, Haiwen Hong, Longtao Huang, Kui Ren

Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents distill past trajectories into reusable experiences. Misleading or adversarial guidance in source trajectories can corrupt these experiences, allowing harmful instructions to persist across tasks and influence future agent actions. Assessing the risks of potentially corrupted experiences poses three challenges: (1) modeling provenance and execution dependencies before experience reuse, (2) learning task-conditioned risk from trajectory outcomes, and (3) repairing risky experiences at online inference. To address these challenges, we present ExpGuard, a framework for retrieval-triggered experience risk assessment and online repair. Specifically, to tackle challenge (1), ExpGuard integrates experience semantics with provenance and execution dependencies in a task-conditioned heterogeneous graph to predict attack and task-failure risks before experience reuse. To enable task-conditioned risk supervision and tackle challenge (2), we conduct matched counterfactual replays of each original experience and its minimally neutralized counterpart from the same task and environment checkpoint, deriving separate risk labels from differences in attack success and legitimate task completion. To tackle challenge (3) at inference time, ExpGuard repairs candidate experiences flagged as high-risk by the graph-based predictor at retrieval and re-predicts their risks before they enter the agent's active experience context. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of ExpGuard in identifying risky experiences and defending agents against experience corruption.

Finally, we conclude with brief initial thoughts on how TRUST principles can extend in autonomous agentic frameworks. Ultimately, the TRUST framework fosters contestability and epistemic grounding as foundational evaluation principles for human-machine workflows and, eventually, autonomous agentic workflows.

Tables published in biomedical research papers, particularly supplemental tables, capture knowledge assertions that may not be in the main text of the manuscript for several reasons, including low statistical significance, space limitations, and narrative scope. These tables include data (e.g., association statistics, assay readouts, curated gene lists, and omics) encoding relationships between biological entities. Tablassert converts such tabular data (Excel, CSV, TSV, Parquet) into knowledge graphs ready for the NCATS Biomedical Data Translator (Fecho et al. 2025). A user declares in YAML how each table's data map to subject-predicate-object-qualifier statements; Tablassert resolves free text (e.g., ''lung cancer'') to standard identifiers, attaches provenance and statistics, and emits graph files conforming to Translator standards (D. Unni, Bruskiewich, and Moxon 2026; D. R. Unni et al. 2022).

##### **Safe Autoformalization: Sufficiency and Necessity with Entailment Graphs**
2611.31571v1 by Umut A. Acar, Eli Goldweber

Autoformalization translates a natural-language statement into a formula that captures its intent, which depends on the statement's context: the background that disambiguates it. LLMs make autoformalization practical, but their probabilistic output carries no formal guarantees. We propose techniques for safe autoformalization that certify one-sided logical adequacy: a formula sufficient for the intent is safe to enforce, and a formula necessary for it is safe to rely on. We use LLMs to generate candidate formulas and order them by logical strength in an Entailment Graph (EG), an ordering a solver decides. Each edge carries a residual and a co-residual, the two adjoints of the entailment order, all fixed by the candidate pool alone. In a context, two searches return the sufficient frontier, the weakest candidates that entail the statement, and the necessary frontier, the strongest candidates the statement entails; each step is one local check against an edge's residual or co-residual. The frontiers bracket the statement and yield the ambiguity residue, which holds exactly of the situations the candidates certify neither as satisfying nor as violating it; the statement is ambiguous precisely when the residue is satisfiable. For natural-language statements and contexts, an LLM entailment oracle decides the checks; it is sound if it accepts only checks that hold, and complete if it accepts every check that holds. We prove that a sound oracle makes the search safe regardless of pool quality, and that a complete one makes the frontiers tight. On security-critical AWS properties, EGs are safe in all 20 cases of an expert-adjudicated study, where majority vote over equivalence clusters and Universal Self-Consistency are unsafe in 25% and 10%. An ablation with the same oracle but no graph is unsafe in 25% and rejects every candidate in another 30%. We machine-checked our proofs in Lean.

##### **Denoising Diffusion with Progressive Posterior Sampling**
2611.31558v1 by Zeyu Luo, Shuai Huang

Diffusion models provide effective generative priors for solving noisy linear inverse problems. However, existing posterior sampling methods may require singular value decomposition (SVD) of the measurement operator or prior knowledge of the measurement noise variance, while guidance based on local gradients provides only a limited view of posterior geometry. We propose progressive posterior sampling (PPS) with manifold projection, which automatically estimates the measurement noise variance and avoids SVD. During reverse diffusion, PPS constructs a Gaussian approximation to the posterior distribution in closed form and guides a sample drawn from the local prior toward the approximate posterior mean. We then project the guided sample onto a spherical level set that approximates the local noisy image manifold, promoting consistency with the local geometry of the diffusion prior. Experiments on FFHQ and ImageNet across four restoration tasks and three measurement noise levels demonstrate competitive reconstruction accuracy and perceptual quality by PPS.

##### **SpatialCraft: Evolving Procedural Intelligence from Experience and Skills for Spatial Reasoning Agents**
2611.31475v1 by Xiwei Liu, Yulong Li, Xuhui Li, Zhixiang Lu, Haolin Yang, Jianxu Chen, Yifan Lu, Di Fan, Yutong Xie

Spatial reasoning agents increasingly rely on external tools, such as 3D reconstruction, segmentation, and geometric computation, to answer questions that a multimodal large language model (MLLM) cannot resolve from pixels alone. Tool access, however, shifts the bottleneck to orchestration: the agent must decide which tools to call, in what order, and how to interpret their outputs, yet a stateless agent repeats the same mistakes across tasks. We ask whether a frozen tool-using agent can improve its orchestration from its own interaction history, without parameter updates. We present SpatialCraft, a self-evolving framework that maintains two forms of external knowledge: Experiences, condition-action tactics distilled by contrasting rollouts of the same task, and Skills, multi-step procedures with explicit activation and termination conditions. Three designs couple the two streams. Experiences can be anchored to a Skill, appearing only while it is active rather than once per task. A Contrastive Utility Score estimates each Experience's effect by randomly withholding it across rollouts of the same task, separating its contribution from task difficulty, and Experiences with consistently positive utility are consolidated into the Skills they support. Across five spatial benchmarks and four backbones, SpatialCraft achieves the best accuracy in all 20 settings, showing that a frozen agent can learn to orchestrate spatial tools from its own experience.

##### **Leaks by Installments: Cross-Session Answer Disclosure in LLM Tutors and a Ledger-Based Defense**
2611.31429v1 by Venkata Muttineni, Karthik Narayan, Christopher David

Tutors built on large language models (LLMs) are told not to give away answers, but a learner does not have to ask for the answer all at once. Existing safeguards judge each reply within the current conversation, so a learner can open a new session, reword the problem, or request a worked solution to the same problem with different numbers. We call this cross-session disclosure and propose a defense: a persistent, per-learner disclosure ledger. For every problem a learner brings, the system builds a typed solution graph. Before a reply is released, a gate matches the reply's own content, not the learner's wording, against the ledger by result values and by type-labeled solution structure, then computes which steps the learner could now derive. The reply is withheld if that set reaches the final answer or exceeds a budget. In a simulation of 3,000 problems, the ledger reduced leakage through renumbered practice variants from 100% to 10.6% while honest learners completed 94-99.5% of problems. With two real LLM tutors (Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct and Phi-3.5-mini-instruct) on 60 problems, leakage through renumbered variants fell from 0.58 to 0.08 and from 1.00 to 0.18, with 98% honest completion. The ledger did not reduce split requests beyond a per-session gate in the live runs. All acceptance criteria were fixed before each run.

##### **Learning What vs. Learning How: Decomposing Task and Interface Competence in Agentic Training**
2611.31427v1 by Chaeeun Lee, Hossein A. Rahmani, Aryo Pradipta Gema, Kexin Xu, Emine Yilmaz, Pasquale Minervini, T. Ian Simpson, Chen Jin, Amrutha Saseendran

LLM agents operate through harnesses that define system prompts, action formats, feedback loops, and resource constraints. A model may possess the knowledge needed to solve a task yet fail to express that knowledge through a particular harness. This raises a basic question: when a policy fails under a given harness, is it missing task knowledge or the competence to operate the interface? We distinguish two components of agentic competence: task competence, the knowledge and reasoning needed to solve a task, and interface competence, the ability to act on that knowledge through a given harness. Across four reasoning and agentic benchmarks, we show that harness-conditioned training yields vastly different outcomes depending on how these interact. Multi-harness distillation aggregates task knowledge from trajectories collected through different harnesses into a shared policy, but this knowledge is realised only when training preserves or improves competence on the inference-time interface. Otherwise, single-harness training can induce interference in interface competence, sometimes catastrophically collapsing performance on a held-out harness despite improvements in underlying task knowledge. To disentangle task and interface supervision, we introduce harness rehearsal: distillation on traces that carry minimal task knowledge while exercising the target interface. Cross-harness task supervision or rehearsal alone yields little gain, but their combination yields gains far larger than either: on BFCL V3 multi-turn with Qwen3-4B, they achieve 8.5% and 32.5% respectively, versus 58.8% together. Finally, on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is more robust than distillation to single-harness training: it does not substantially degrade performance on other harnesses, and interface competence acquired through multi-harness distillation persists through subsequent RL.

##### **ToolSmith: An Automatic Tool-Generation Framework for Multimodal Search Agents**
2611.31198v1 by Rui Sun, Junhua Huang, Luca Zancato, Md Mofijul Islam, Kai-Wei Chang

Recent multimodal search agents use reasoning, vision, and live web search across many turns to answer questions beyond parametric knowledge by orchestrating a small library of hand-crafted search and visual tools. However, the library is laborious to create and maintain, and frozen when new query types, evidence formats, and failure modes are encountered in the wild. Human practitioners, by contrast, cope with insufficient tools by fashioning new ones from familiar parts and reusing them across tasks. Inspired by this, we propose ToolSmith, an automatic tool-generation framework that grows and reuses modules on top of a lean base tool library. We evaluate ToolSmith on three in-domain multimodal search benchmarks and ten out-of-domain benchmarks under a VLM-as-a-Judge protocol. ToolSmith consistently improves over the base library across deployed agents of varying scales, with larger backbones reaping greater gains, demonstrating the scalability of the approach. Our code and data are available at https://threesr.github.io/toolsmith/.

##### **SRCWDNet: Semantic-Relational Cross-Window Dual-Graph Network for Multi-Label Remote Sensing Scene Classification**
2611.31135v1 by Fareeha Amir

Multi-label remote sensing scene classification remains a challenging task due to complex spatial-spectral patterns, extreme class imbalance, and inter-label dependencies that are often insufficiently modeled. To address these challenges, we introduce a Semantic-Relational Cross-Window Dual-Graph Network (SRCWDNet), which integrates three key modules: (1) a Cross-Window Attention module that captures local and global visual context through learnable global tokens; (2) a Dual Label-Graph module that adaptively combines statistical co-occurrence graphs and learned semantic-embedding graphs to facilitate label-relational reasoning; and (3) a Neural Classifier Chain that sequentially predicts labels to exploit inter-label dependencies. Evaluated using five-fold cross-validation and trained with an Asymmetric-Graph-Consistency loss, SRCWDNet achieves accuracies of 98.93% and 96.53%, with corresponding F1-scores of 0.9365 and 0.8723 on the MLRSNet and China-MAS50k datasets, respectively, outperforming CNN, transformer, and lightweight baselines. These findings demonstrate that jointly modeling visual attention and adaptive label correlations can substantially improve multi-label remote sensing scene classification, particularly under long-tailed class distributions.

##### **What People Choose Not to Say: A Multimodal Benchmark for Fine-Grained Information Manipulation in Conversations**
2611.31346v1 by Yuhan Bian, Zhaokai Liang, Jia Wang, Haoyu Liu, Yu Chen, Jianfeng Qu, Zhixu Li

Recent advances in MLLMs have improved social understanding, enabling models to recognize emotions and distinguish truthful from deceptive behaviors. However, existing benchmarks largely focus on coarse-grained emotion recognition or binary truth-deception detection, overlooking the diverse ways people manipulate information in conversations, such as withholding, leakage, fabrication, evasion, and defensive counterclaim. Understanding these behaviors requires reasoning about underlying facts, participant knowledge, and conversational context, making them difficult to evaluate reliably. To address this gap, we introduce InfoCon, a multimodal benchmark for fine-grained information manipulation in conversations. InfoCon identifies six fine-grained strategies and derives a coarse consistency label from their factual and interactional relations. We develop an evidence-grounded multi-agent annotation framework to construct and verify reliable labels from rich contextual evidence. We benchmark representative multimodal models and systematically analyze their fine-grained information manipulation understanding, providing a testbed beyond binary truth-deception judgments.

##### **Self-Upgrading Embodied Agents**
2611.31117v1 by Jiajun Fan, Neil He, Qinglong Zheng, Changyi Yang, Tong Wei, Andy Mao, Zetian Li, Chaoran Cheng, Hui Ren, Ruihan Guo, Xianrui Zhong, Xizheng Yu, Jingyuan Li, Xi Zhu, Kaiqu Liang, Chang Xu, Zun Qian, Zidi Xiong, Yuezhu Zhao, Xu Huang, Ge Liu

An embodied agent built on a language model goes to work with the weights it was given and keeps them for life. To get better, it needs more training, or reflection, memory, search or prompts that cost every step and are lost when the task changes. Editing weights by reasoning, weight-space programming, could end this, but has stayed with models that only reason or relied on a far larger commercial model. We introduce the self-upgrading embodied agent, to our knowledge the first that learns from its own experience by upgrading its own weights on the job, between its tasks, with no training, no gradient, no reward model, no second model, no test-time scaling and no harness or prompt engineering. By day a 27B agent works in a household environment. At night it reads its record, finds many of its steps wasted on replies that reach the token limit before any action, traces the habit to one direction in its hidden states, and rewrites one tensor of its weights. An agent that rewrites itself must first never break itself, so it is its own examiner: it tests every change against its unchanged self on practice tasks and keeps only what holds. When an edit holds, the next day it is a better agent, at the same cost per step: over thousands of held-out episodes, 48 fresh seeds a task, its one kept edit makes it complete significantly more tasks, in fewer steps, with fewer wasted and fewer tokens. This self-improvement is multi-round, stable and continual: through the nights that follow, the upgrade holds, without collapse. Of four sizes of its family, only the 27B agent does this. From here on, an embodied agent need not keep training and inference apart: as it works, it gathers its own experience and upgrades itself, with no separate pre-training or post-training and no human intervention. We believe this opens lifelong continual learning for robots: stable, continual, recursive self-improvement.

##### **RUIBench: What Makes a Good Robot-Use Interface?**
2611.31115v1 by Yanzhe Chen, Zhijun Cao, Zechen Bai, Wenzheng Zeng, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Yiqi Lin, Guoqiang Liang, Xiaokang Liu, Ke Sun, Kevin Qinghong Lin, Mike Zheng Shou

Foundation models can reason about complex physical tasks, yet translating this intelligence into reliable robot execution remains challenging. We argue that a critical, often overlooked factor is the robot-use interface (RUI), which determines how agents express and execute physical actions. To systematically study its impact, we introduce RUIBench, comprising 30 manipulation tasks across five suites spanning world knowledge, in-context learning, discovery, dynamic interaction, and contact-rich manipulation. We further uncover a verification gap, where agents frequently declare success despite observable failures, revealing the need for task-level verification beyond individual actions. ##### **AlphaDefect: Expert-Level AI for Slay the Spire 2**
2611.31076v1 by Qiyue Chen, Taian Yu, Junpei Komiyama, Haobo Huang

Slay the Spire 2 (STS2) is a single-player deck-building game that requires long-term planning under heavy randomness. We present AlphaDefect, an expert-level AI system for STS2: for each character, one network trained with proximal policy optimization (PPO) makes every decision of a run, in combat and outside it. At the highest difficulty, Ascension 10, on two versions of the game current in November 2026 and on test seeds not played in training, AlphaDefect wins 62-77% of runs across six characters with greedy play and 68-80% with test-time search, after 1.3 to 6.7 billion decisions of training per character; runs uploaded by human players of all levels are won 11-20% of the time at the same difficulty. The networks read the game as entity records that identify each card, relic and enemy through a learned embedding and attributes from the game data, together with consequences of public information computed by the engine. Ablations show how much the agent depends on this encoding: without these identities, the network did not win a single run, and without the computed consequences it won 8.3 points fewer. To our knowledge, AlphaDefect is the strongest publicly reported STS2 agent as of November 2026.

##### **From Local Errors to System Failures: Modeling and Benchmarking Error Propagation in Multi-Agent LLM Systems**
2611.31063v1 by Wenlong Yu, Bingxu Lu, Qilong Wang, Qinghua Hu, Qianru Sun

In multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs), errors can propagate through intermediate messages and compromise final answers. ##### **Unpacking the Evaluators: How Configuration Shapes the Evaluation of Alignment in Explainable AI**
2611.30816v1 by Gizem Karagoz, Tanir Ozcelebi, Nirvana Meratnia

Explainable AI (XAI) evaluation fundamentally requires human-centered evaluation, assessing whether explanations align with human reasoning and expert knowledge. However, such evaluations often require expert-driven manual validation and are difficult to scale, leading to the widespread use of alignment-based metrics, where attribution maps are compared against predefined ground-truth (GT) representations. Despite their popularity, alignment evaluations are implicitly bias towards multiple design choices made during the evaluation process, for example GT representations, metric families, metric formulations (soft vs. hard), and thresholding strategies. In this paper, we formalize alignment evaluation as a structured evaluation configuration space through which the influence of these design choices can be systematically analyzed. In addition, we propose a novel taxonomy of XAI output behavior based on local entropy and boundary mass ratio, enabling structured characterization of attribution patterns for alignment evaluation. Our experiments reveal systematic flows (triples of XAI-GT-metric configurations), showing that alignment outcomes provide distinct behavior patterns under certain configurations, such as soft/hard metric implementations. We further observe varying levels of threshold sensitivity across metric families and attribution structures. These findings demonstrate that alignment evaluation cannot be treated as a fixed or invariant measure of explanation quality, and provide practical guidance for designing more reliable and behavior-aware XAI evaluation protocols.

##### **PVFormer: Proper Velocity Transformer for Stable and Scalable Hyperbolic Representation Learning**
2611.30785v1 by Xianglong Shi, Nicu Sebe, Bernhard Schölkopf, Ziheng Chen

Hyperbolic representations have shown remarkable success on hierarchical and tree-like data, and Transformers have become a central architecture for representation learning. Existing hyperbolic Transformer designs are typically built in the Poincaré or Lorentz models, where bounded domains, time-like constraints, tangent-space detours, or projection steps make it difficult to keep the full block intrinsic and scalable. To address this gap, we propose PVFormer, to the best of our knowledge, the first Transformer architecture that operates natively in proper velocity (PV) space. Extensive experiments support the effectiveness of PVFormer across graph, text, and vision benchmarks.

#### Abstracts
##### **Collect for Coverage, Replay with Evidence: Adaptive and Efficient On-Policy Distillation for Long-Horizon Agents**
2611.34756v1 by Haowen Hou, Gen Li, Ruilin Li, Yicheng Liu, Zhen Huang, Shuai Dong, Congcong Wang, Kaiwen Tuo, Hongrui Wu, Zelin Peng, Kanghui Tian, Ji Zhao, Dianyi Wang, Jiaqi Wang

Fully online on-policy distillation (OPD) is costly for long-horizon agents because each update depends on fresh environment rollouts. Prefix replay offers an efficient alternative by sampling current-student responses from stored histories, reducing idle time for long-tail rollouts and allowing each collected trajectory to support multiple updates. However, our analysis of the complete prefix replay pipeline identifies two limitations in long-horizon settings. In trajectory collection, teacher-only trajectories miss student-induced histories, while broader history coverage can reduce teacher reliability. In trajectory reuse, fixed position-based schedules cannot identify critical decisions that may occur at any trajectory depth. We introduce CARE-OPD (Collect for coverAge, Replay with Evidence) to address both limitations. Student-seeded teacher completions and response-level confidence gating expand history coverage while filtering unreliable updates. Hindsight replay allocation then uses downstream execution evidence to select consequential turns. Across long-horizon tasks Software Engineering and Deep Research, CARE-OPD outperforms online OPD and prior prefix replay baselines. On SWE-bench Verified, it raises task success to 59.5% while reducing end-to-end training time by 45% and environment actions by 44% relative to asynchronous online OPD.

摘要：

##### **Sage-Med: Scaling Medical Multimodal Intelligence**
2611.34575v1 by Sage-Med Team,  :, Ming Hu, Cheng Tang, Wei Li, Rongbin Li, Feilong Tang, Junru Hu, Hongze Zhu, Qiujuan Yan, Huanze Tang, Shaocong Wei, Siyuan Yan, Min Cen, Zhongxing Xu, Zhonghua Wang, Lie Ju, Zhen Yu, Wenjie Li, Shiyan Su, Jing Hao, Jingyong Liang, Diping Song, Fang Yan, Chenglong Ma, Junzhi Ning, Haochen Xue, Zanting Ye, Chenrun Wang, Jinjie Wei, Yi Xin, Jianghan Shen, Siqi Luo, Jiyao Liu, Huidong Xie, Jun Zhao, Rulin Zhou, Longquan Jiang, Ziyan Huang, Ying Chen, Hanxiao Zhang, Junyang Wu, Mouxiao Bian, Jiashi Lin, Chenyu Wang, Qi Qin, Jie Huang, Yiling Zhou, Jiawei Chen, Kefan Wang, Jingjing Hua, Yaoyu Liu, Huihui Xu, Tianbin Li, Jin Ye, Ziyang Chen, Shujian Gao, Zilin Lu, Jiangtao Yan, Changkai Ji, Wenhao Tang, Xinyi Zeng, Yuxing Chen, Hongwei Yang, Rongzhao Zhang, Jinru Ding, Yingcheng Wu, Zhenfei Yin, Jinzhe Li, Lu Lu, Yun Zhong, Zhishan Lin, Bingli Wang, Xiaohan Ren, Jianmin Qian, Penghao Zhao, Zhongbo Tian, Zhenbang Zhou, Peiling Dai, Mengjing Zhao, Jingyu Zheng, Pinan Liu, Shiwei Li, Victoria Mar, Cristina Vico-Alonso, Qiang Wu, Xianming Liu, Lingtong Huang, Dili Song, Minghui Zhang, Wenlong Zhang, Qipeng Guo, Yuqiang Li, Nanqing Dong, Yun Gu, Qi Duan, Hongliang Ren, Hongming Shan, Yin Xie, Xiang An, Benyou Wang, Yueming Su, Yefeng Zheng, Zongyuan Ge, Chun-Mei Feng, Jie Xu, Yu Qiao, Junjun He, Yirong Chen

Establishing effective scaling laws for medical multimodal large language models (MLLMs) remains a persistent and fundamental challenge. Our systematic evaluation shows that existing medical MLLMs tend to underperform frontier general-purpose MLLMs across a range of medical tasks, while scaling model size primarily improves text capabilities rather than multimodal perception, and enabling reasoning modes fails to close this visual gap. To investigate how medical multimodal intelligence scales in practice, we introduce Sage-Med-Preview, a 35B-parameter medical multimodal model with 3B activated parameters, built by combining stage-specific curation over 322.7 million candidate records with a progressive training curriculum spanning medical pretraining, staged supervised fine-tuning, and adaptive reinforcement learning. Across this curriculum, progressive supervised refinement and adaptive reinforcement learning yield consistent stage-wise gains, substantially advancing both specialized visual understanding and medical language capabilities. Evaluated against 77 competing models across 238 clinical tasks spanning 61 public and 13 private multi-center benchmarks in more than 40 modalities, Sage-Med-Preview achieves an average score of 58.26% on the 31 public multimodal benchmarks. It outperforms the state-of-the-art open-source medical model Hulu-Med-235B-A22B at 50.99% and the general scientific model Intern-S2-Preview at 52.24%, and approaches the advanced proprietary model GPT-6.2-Sol across multiple tasks such as comprehensive multimodal understanding, report generation, and visual grounding. Although substantial challenges remain in complex medical reasoning and multi-step clinical workflows, this report provides empirical evidence, actionable data and training recipes, and a rigorous benchmark toward scalable clinical multimodal intelligence.

摘要：

##### **EPPC-CARE: Staged Hierarchical Annotation of Communication Behaviors in Secure Patient-Provider Messages**
2611.34539v1 by Samah Fodeh, Sreeraj Ramachandran, Ganesh Puthiaraju, Sarah Schellhorn

Objectives: Patient portal messages provide invaluable information about communication dynamics between patients and healthcare providers, but extracting it manually is labor-intensive. We developed and evaluated EPPC-CARE, an automated framework for identifying communication behaviors together with the supporting text for each classification. Materials and Methods: From 867 messages, we obtained 5,516 reference standard annotations. Each annotation is a triplet of communication-related text (span), a broader communication category (Code), and a specific subcategory (Sub-code). We evaluated EPPC-CARE's identified triplets across six open-weight large language models (LLMs) against supervised fine-tuning and communication-based models such as EPPC-OASIS. We also compared EPPC-CARE with human expert annotations on 100 messages. Results: EPPC-CARE improved performance over EPPC-OASIS across all six models. EPPC-CARE's Triplet F1 improved by 2.90 percentage points on average, with a best score of 64.4%. On the human expert evaluation subset, EPPC-CARE and the human expert showed comparable agreement with the reference standard, with differences favoring EPPC-CARE. Discussion: These findings suggest that identifying triplet components (span, code, subcode) sequentially can improve performance across model families while preserving the supporting text behind each identification. EPPC-CARE links the communication code and subcode with the source text (span), supporting human verification of automated annotations in clinical informatics. EPPC-CARE may therefore make larger-scale studies of patient-provider communication more feasible and provide a foundation for human-in-the-loop annotation workflows. Conclusion: EPPC-CARE offers a more reliable and reviewable approach for automated characterization of communication behaviors in secure patient-provider messaging.

摘要：

##### **MedSkill-E$^{2}$: Evaluating and Evolving Skills for Medical Agents**
2611.34250v1 by Jingjing Hua, Shaocong Wei, Jianghan Shen, Junzhi Ning, Min Cen, Cheng Tang, Ziyan Huang, Jiepeng Li, Fengshuo Liu, Kangkun Mao, Mouxiao Bian, Yirong Chen, Ming Hu, Jiyao Liu

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills to access domain knowledge and complete real-world tasks. We observe that even when a skill specifies the rules and procedures needed for a task, agents may fail to apply them effectively during autonomous execution, a phenomenon we call skill Enable But Underuse (EBU). This issue is particularly consequential in medical tasks, where overlooking a single critical constraint can yield a plausible but incorrect result. To evaluate and address EBU, we introduce MedSkill-E$^{2}$, a framework for evaluating and evolving skills for medical agents. We construct EBU-Bench, a challenging benchmark covering 12 medically relevant task groups and associated skills, to evaluate agents' ability to use available skills autonomously. We quantify the EBU gap as the difference in skill-use accuracy between autonomous use and explicitly guided reading and application of the same skill. On EBU-Bench, all evaluated model-harness combinations exhibit EBU, with a mean gap of 43.94 percentage points (pp). For the same model, skill-use accuracy under autonomous use differs by up to 38.96 pp across harnesses. Motivated by these observations, we propose EBU-Evolve, which uses execution feedback from autonomous and explicitly guided skill use to distill effective guidance into reusable skill instructions. Relative to their pre-evolution versions, the revised skills improve skill-use accuracy during autonomous execution by 33.33 to 55.17 pp and continue to yield improvements when transferred across multiple models and harnesses. Our results show that autonomous skill-use accuracy varies across harnesses with the model and skill held fixed, and model rankings can change. EBU can persist even after a skill is opened. EBU-Evolve improves autonomous skill-use accuracy on unseen tasks within the same skill, with gains transferring across models and harnesses.

摘要：

##### **Did the Model Forecast It, or Already Know It? Evaluating Large Language Models for Novel Drug-Target Prioritisation with a Temporal Knowledge-Graph Oracle**
2611.34247v1 by Pui Chung Siu, Yuan Liang, Claudia Cabrera, Mani Mudaliar, Arkaitz Zubiaga

AI systems increasingly help decide which drug targets enter costly clinical trials. We recast each target-disease hypothesis as a dated event, the pair's first clinical trial. This lets us test whether a language model can forecast such events after its training cutoff, rather than recall events before it. We rebuild a dated biomedical knowledge graph as it stood at the end of each year from 2020 up to its newest version. We then ask six open language models, with and without this graph, which targets will enter a first trial for a disease the next year. Without retrieval, the models rank about as well as trial history, a count of each target's past trials in the same therapeutic area, also after their training cutoffs. Evidence dated before the decision adds at most a small gain, before or after the models' training cutoffs. The newest graph raises five of six models clearly above that baseline, but its paths usually include the answer: the pair's own trial, dated after the decision. Evaluations of target prioritisation should date both features and labels, with evidence from before the decision and outcomes after it.

摘要：

##### **Weakly Conditioned Diffusion Model Augmentation for Prostate Segmentation**
2611.34230v1 by Chibike Ugbam, Muhammad Bilal, Jia Wu, Shafaq Murad, Rizwan Qureshi, Hazrat Ali

Deep learning models for prostate MRI segmentation often struggle to generalize when trained on limited heterogeneous data, leading to boundary errors and over-segmentation. We present an augmentation framework using mask-conditioned synthetic and diverse T2-weighted MRI volumes to improve boundary precision and reduce over-segmentation. We employ a 2.5D weakly mask-conditioned denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) via channel concatenation. The diffusion model conditions on stacks of adjacent binary masks from training folds. The model generates T2 volumes consistent with the conditioning anatomy while diversifying tissue appearance. We evaluate the proposed approach on two cohorts of data: (i) the LUND probe cohort (n=432) for strategy selection and training nnU-Net from scratch with a 23% synthetic data mix, and (ii) the Medical Segmentation Decathlon (MSD) prostate cohort (n=32) evaluated with a 20% synthetic data mix. For the LUND cohort, we found that training an nnU-Net model from scratch with a 23% synthetic data mix reduced the mean false-positive voxels per case from 11,102 (baseline) to 10,757. On the MSD cohort, the augmented model with a 20% synthetic mix achieved a mean cross-validated Dice of 0.9094, improving over the baseline (0.8935) by 1.59 percentage points and reducing mean false-positive voxels by 23.1% (4,201 to 3,229). Experimental results on both these cohorts suggest that mask-conditioned DDPM augmentation can reduce over-segmentation errors and improve boundary adherence in data-constrained settings.

##### **Multi-view fnirs identifies neurophysiological subtypes in post-stroke aphasia**
2611.34169v1 by Zijian Kang, Bingbing Lin, Jinglei Ni, Meifang Tian, Wai Ting Siok, Jia Huang, Nizhuan Wang

Post-stroke aphasia is clinically heterogeneous, and conventional classifications may not capture differences in brain functional organisation. We tested whether resting-state and task-based functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) could identify neurophysiological subgroups alongside clinical assessment.Seventy-nine patients underwent unsupervised clustering using three fNIRS feature views: resting-state activity and connectivity, language-task activation, and rest-to-task connectivity reconfiguration. Similarity network fusion and spectral clustering integrated the views. Clinical and behavioural measures were excluded from clustering.Three clusters (n = 32, 17 and 30) were identified. This solution had the largest eigengap and was the only candidate significant after permutation testing and Holm correction (adjusted P = 0.001). It was stable under subsampling (mean adjusted Rand index, 0.773), but agreement fell to 0.217 when the resting-state view was removed. Clusters differed in most regional intrinsic-activity measures and all resting-state connectivity summaries, with few differences in task activation or connectivity reconfiguration. Their profiles showed higher right anterior prefrontal activity, distributed fronto-sensorimotor and parietal alterations, and reduced interhemispheric connectivity, respectively.Cluster membership was not significantly associated with conventional aphasia subtype (Cramér's V = 0.287), and baseline clinical language scores and task performance did not differ between clusters.These findings identify three neurophysiological subgroups distinguished mainly by intrinsic activity and resting-state functional connectivity, not clearly aligned with conventional aphasia classifications or baseline clinical measures. Multi-view fNIRS analysis may complement clinical characterisation, requiring independent and longitudinal validation.

##### **EGSE: Evidence-Grounded Skill Evolution for Medical Reasoning Agents**
2611.33909v1 by Mingyuan Li, Tong Jia, Ziyuan Yang, Wenbo Qi, Haoran Wang, Jie Gui, Baosheng Yu

Medical reasoning agents combine foundation models with specialized tools to interpret medical images, yet reusing their historical experience remains challenging. A correct final answer can conceal flawed reasoning or tool use, synthesized advice may lack source support, and superficially similar cases may require different diagnostic procedures. In this paper, we propose EGSE, an evidence-grounded skill evolution framework that distills historical dialogues from similar cases into reusable conditional workflows and progressively refines a multi-faceted skill library through supported and validated updates to improve medical agents. EGSE evaluates intermediate reasoning and tool use and contrasts repeated successful and failed rollouts to extract useful practices and evidence-supported avoidance guidance. These experiences are synthesized into workflows with applicability conditions and checks traceable to source dialogues. Library additions and merges require source-grounded review and execution validation. At inference time, EGSE routes queries to skill nodes organized by imaging modality, question task type, and disease category, then retrieves and selects applicable workflows based on their conditions, the current images, and the question to guide evidence acquisition and interpretation. Experiments across four chest X-ray benchmarks show that EGSE outperforms representative medical agents and skill evolution methods in reasoning accuracy while reducing tool calls. It also delivers consistent accuracy gains across seven foundation models and supports skill transfer across datasets.

##### **EATS: Scaling Tool-Use Experience for Medical Reasoning Agents**
2611.33897v1 by Mingyuan Li, Tong Jia, Haoran Wang, Wenbo Qi, Ziyuan Yang, Bowen Ma, Jie Gui, Baosheng Yu

Medical reasoning agents combine large language model (LLM) reasoning with clinical workflows and specialized neural tools. However, general-purpose LLMs receive limited training exposure to domain-specific medical tools, and the uncertainty and distributional shifts of neural tool outputs make selecting tools from simple descriptions significantly more challenging than in deterministic settings such as calculators or dictionary lookups. In this paper, we propose EATS, an experience-augmented tool selection strategy that leverages accumulated tool-use trajectories to improve tool selection in medical reasoning agents. EATS constructs a memory of past interactions, including queries, tool choices, arguments, and self-evaluated feedback, and retrieves relevant experiences to guide tool selection for new queries. As memory construction does not require ground-truth supervision, EATS scales naturally with test-time experience. We evaluate EATS on tool-augmented medical reasoning tasks, including closed-ended visual question answering and free-form report generation. Experiments on four public datasets, ChestAgentBench, CheXbench, MIMIC-CXR, and SLAKE-VQA, across three multimodal LLMs, Qwen3-VL 2B, Qwen3-VL 32B, and GPT-5.5, show consistent gains in tool selection behavior and downstream medical reasoning performance. Code is available at https://github.com/Limingyuan001/EATS.

##### **Can Its Own Misses Teach an MLLM to Find Small Brain Metastases?**
2611.33836v1 by Yixuan Duan, Kexin Geng, Hanwen Xu, Wei Qiu

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are becoming general readers of medical images, yet they remain weak at locating small abnormalities. Brain metastases expose this gap: at 384 px a 0.1 mL lesion covers less than a quarter of one visual token, and at one false positive per scan frontier MLLMs find fewer than 30% of lesions of 0.1-1 mL. Fine-tuning on one dataset helps but transfers poorly, and improving it further is slow trial and error steered by a single score. We introduce LANTERN (Lesion-finding AgeNt Through Error-driven Recursive learNing), a framework in which an MLLM improves recursively from its own misses. LANTERN recasts model development as an error-driven loop: a failure census attributes every missed lesion to one of four causes, an LLM mentor revises one of four levels (evidence acquisition, visual representation, learning policy, decision aggregation) per round, and a rule frozen before the first round decides what is kept, without test data and with every tool call audited. Over 15 unattended rounds, LANTERN raises a fine-tuned 4B MLLM from finding 75% to 94% of lesions at one false positive per scan on BraTS-MET, and 91% of those of 0.1-1 mL, compared with at most 51% and 29% for five frontier MLLMs read under the starting protocol; the gains hold on an external cohort (89%) and on unseen meningiomas (77%). These results show that an MLLM's own misses, attributed to their causes, can drive its reliable recursive self-improvement.

##### **RadOncGym: An Agent-Based Framework for Radiotherapy Dose-Mimicking Optimization**
2611.33821v1 by Tong Miao, Shupeng Cheng, Xiwen Chen, Ruishan Liu

AI dose-prediction models generate patient-specific 3D dose distributions from patient anatomy, providing an important intermediate representation for automated radiotherapy planning. However, an AI-predicted dose must undergo downstream optimization to produce a physically constrained treatment plan, a process that requires balancing competing planning objectives. We present RadOncGym, an agent-based framework that enables an LLM agent to iteratively tune 12 planning-objective weights for dose-mimicking optimization. To reduce iterative evaluation cost, RadOncGym incorporates a surrogate verifier that provides rapid feedback while allowing the agent to decide when to request a full planning solve within a fixed solve budget. We evaluate RadOncGym on 60 OpenKBP head-and-neck patients, each paired with 10 predictions from OpenKBP-Opt dose-prediction sets, yielding 600 patient-prediction contexts per experimental configuration. Under RadOncGym's 16-turn setting with a three-solve budget, a GPT-5.6 Sol agent reduces mean clinical-criterion violation by 0.6131 Gy and improves DVH and dose scores by 0.5986 and 0.2204 Gy relative to leaving all 12 weights fixed at 1, outperforming all evaluated non-agent weight-tuning methods under the same solve budget. It remains competitive with the 16-solve variants of these methods while requiring 75.0-77.6% less normalized runtime. Code and configurations are available at https://github.com/miaot-usc/RadOncGym.

##### **What, Who, and When: Structured Harm Assessment for Mental Health Safety via Short Reasoning**
2611.33263v1 by Catarina Botelho, António Farinhas, John Mendonça, Areti Vassilopoulos, Inês Gonçalves, Maya D'Eon, Hugo Pitorro, Laura Cabello, Carrie Spadine, Nuno M. Guerreiro, Ricardo Rei

The growing use of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support raises important safety concerns. Existing guard models primarily identify categories of harmful content, often overlooking the context needed to determine an appropriate response. We introduce a clinically grounded taxonomy that characterizes harm along three dimensions: what the harm is, who is involved, and when it occurs. It further separates harm characterization from intervention decisions, thus enabling safety policies to be specified outside the model weights through explicit, clinician-curated instructions that can be updated and audited. We validate the taxonomy with expert clinicians and validate a panel of LLM judges to scale annotation. Relabeling public benchmarks in this taxonomy shows that they rarely contain harm directed at the user, which accounts for nearly half of the harm in real conversations. We then train MindGuard2, a 35B-A3B reasoning-based guard model using supervised fine-tuning on short reasoning traces followed by reinforcement learning with a reasoning-length penalty, to minimize inference overhead while preserving the benefits of reasoning. On two public benchmarks and real-world conversations, MindGuard2 achieves balanced accuracy comparable to the substantially larger GLM-5.3 at approximately 10x lower median latency, advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier. Furthermore, it achieves low false-positive rates at high recall, helping balance the need to detect unsafe situations against the risk of unnecessary interventions that may disrupt support and undermine user trust. We release a safety guardrail model with SOTA performance trained on synthetic data, together with synthetic conversations and annotations of public benchmarks in our taxonomy.

Interactions between patients and health systems as well as conditions that individuals experience during a disease are represented in patient journeys. Therefore, patient journeys are important assets to investigate how care is being provided, and how health systems can be designed to better suit the needs of patients. While there is initial work on patient journeys from a process mining perspective with traditional case notions, object-centric process mining techniques have yet to be covered. This paper addresses this gap by investigating object-centric event log (OCEL) generation from patient journeys. ##### **Can LLM Judges Catch Unsafe Clinical Actions? A Fault-Injection Study**
2611.33012v1 by Neda Ghafouri

As clinical AI agents begin to interact with electronic health records (EHRs), large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate their actions. But how reliably can these judges recognize clinically dangerous mistakes, especially when an agent appears to have completed its task successfully? We investigate this question using MedAgentBench by introducing controlled clinical errors into verified agent trajectories. The experiments include 416 faulty trajectories, eight error categories, and six open-weight LLM judges. By comparing each faulty trajectory with its correct counterpart, we measure whether judges genuinely recognize errors rather than simply flagging more actions as unsafe. The findings show that access to clinical evidence significantly improves judge reliability. When shown only the final answer, judges cannot distinguish errors that leave it unchanged, while flagging 56% of correct trajectories. With full trajectory information, the ability to distinguish unsafe actions from correct ones reaches a score of 0.75, and providing actual EHR responses improves discrimination by 0.39. Surprisingly, including the agent's reasoning does not improve overall performance and reduces detection of incorrectly reported clinical values by 0.18, which remain the hardest errors to detect. MedGemma performed worse than the general-purpose model and was the only judge to miss potentially dangerous medication overdoses. We also find that the benchmark's original grader accepts all 38 trajectories with an intentionally skipped laboratory check, while combining LLM judges with a rule-based checker informed by the full EHR record detects 97% of injected faults.

2611.32657v1 by Wenjie Deng, Zijian Tu, Xueyin Zhou, Huayi Wang, Zhuoyun Du, Mingyu Chen, Haochao Ying, Jian Wu

Surgical resection planning centers on generating executable resection surfaces from anatomical evidence and operative constraints. Medical multimodal foundation models enable anatomical understanding, and segmentation models predict resection regions, but neither translates these capabilities into explicitly constrained, executable resection surfaces. We present GeoSurge, an anatomy-grounded framework that combines mask-derived metrics with structure-aware point-cloud tokens to generate executable, editable resection surfaces. To make resection geometry learnable and editable, GeoSurge maps actions to parametric geometry through a surface-action grammar. Using this grammar, anatomy-oriented and tumor-centered templates generate candidates, which are filtered by hard constraints and semantic deduplication. ##### **KiRA: Kronecker Input-Gated Rank Adaptation for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning of Pretrained Models**
2611.33236v1 by Akil Ahmad Taki, Shaikh Anowarul Fattah

Full fine-tuning of large pretrained models is expensive, and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) is the standard alternative. Most PEFT methods use low-rank updates, whose rank lies far below that of full fine-tuning and is the same for every input. Input-adaptive variants vary the capacity per input but remain within the low-rank bottleneck, and whether a task benefits from input conditioning is not known before training. This paper introduces Kronecker input-gated rank adaptation (KiRA). Instead of the conventional low-rank product, the update $ΔW$ is the Kronecker product of a frozen random $p\times p$ matrix $A$ and a trainable factor $M(x)$, which overcomes the rank ceiling: because rank is multiplicative under this product, $A$ multiplies the rank of $M(x)$ by $p$ without adding trainable parameters. The factor $M(x)$ is further decomposed as $B_1\,\mathrm{diag}(g(x))\,B_2$, where the input gate $g(x)$, computed once per sample by a network shared across all adapted layers, adjusts the rank of the update to each input in steps of $p$. Where input conditioning is unnecessary, the gate is folded into $B_1$ at deployment. Because the gate changes the rank only in discrete steps, the benefit of input conditioning can be predicted before training from a few hundred labeled inputs. Across six medical imaging and language benchmarks, KiRA attains the highest mean score against five published baselines with 8.7--9.7 times fewer trainable parameters than low-rank adaptation, and the pre-training criterion agrees with the measured benefit of input conditioning on all six tasks.

##### **JECT: Addressing the Context Shortcut in Joint-Embedding Predictive Learning for Chest CT**
2611.31849v1 by Hanbin Ko, Hong-Jun Choi, Jihun Kim, Young-Gon Kim, Dongheon Lee, Chang Min Park

Joint-embedding predictive architectures learn by predicting features of masked regions from visible context. In chest computed tomography, the findings that decide a diagnosis, such as a small nodule, are often not predictable from the surrounding anatomy. A hidden finding is then best predicted toward normal anatomy, and the prediction loss stops rewarding the encoder for representing it. We call this the context shortcut. Conditioning the predictor on the radiology report repairs the prediction, but in our setup the encoder gains little over image-only pretraining: the report supplies what the encoder never had to learn. JECT therefore gives the report two roles: a condition for the predictor and, through masked section-text prediction, a target for the encoder, which must predict the report's anatomical sections from the unmasked scan. A hierarchical three-dimensional encoder that predicts at every stage keeps this clinical content at every scale. In ablations, the text target improves classification and localization, while conditioning adds little to the encoder. Trained from scratch on 173k volumes, the recipe reaches the level of current computed tomography foundation models in classification, retrieval and nodule malignancy, and of task-specific models in lung-cancer risk prediction after fine-tuning. Conditioning still matters: its predictor can be reused, with no extra training, to show where a report sentence acts, changing most at the finding the sentence describes.
