##### **Rapid Exploratory Maps and their potential role in supporting evidence-to-policy dialogues and processes**
2611.31693v1 by Gareth J. Hollands, Ian Shemilt, James Thomas

Producing useful evidence syntheses requires clearly formulated research questions linked to an understanding of their optimal scope. When policymakers (or other end users) commission or steer the production of systematic reviews or other evidence syntheses to fulfil a demand for evidence, these parameters are rarely specified sufficiently for production to proceed. Instead, these details are typically developed via a collaborative dialogic process involving evidence producers and users to ensure the resulting synthesis will meet users' needs. This process is informed by scoping and (more-or-less formally) mapping the existing evidence base, which can take many weeks or months, slowed by applying mostly manual processes to identify and characterise the evidence. Recent developments in artificial intelligence (AI) technologies have opened up new opportunities for more rapid, iterative, and responsive mapping of relevant evidence to support such early-stage evidence-to-policy dialogues and processes. In this article, we outline and characterise this emerging approach, termed Rapid Exploratory Maps (REMs). We also present two illustrative case studies drawn from first-hand experiences within a programme of evidence reviews production to inform government policy in England. While this approach could be transformative for the mapping process, its broader potential is as yet largely unassessed, and it confers important challenges and likely risks, as well as benefits. Determining whether REMs could usefully support more efficient ways of working with end users when developing and designing evidence syntheses will require continued development and careful evaluation.

##### **TesseraFlow: Scalable Video Diffusion for Dynamic Medical Imaging Inverse Problems**
2611.29099v1 by Atakan Topcu, Mohammadhossein Momeni, Alfredo De Goyeneche, Michael Lustig

Generative priors are state-of-the-art for modeling medical images in image reconstruction tasks, but reconstruction with video priors takes tens of seconds to minutes per frame. Latent priors inherit the errors of their autoencoder, the memory of pixel-space whole-frame priors grows with the frame size, and pixel-space patch priors are slow for two reasons. First, their deep networks process every frame at full resolution at every sampling step, even though early steps resolve only low frequencies. Second, their samplers spend many network evaluations per sampling step on overlapping windows, multiple re-noising rounds, or backpropagation through the prior. We introduce TesseraFlow, a complex-valued pixel-space flow prior that reduces the first cost. A global encoder reads a low-resolution view of every frame once per sampling step. A single shallow decoder operates on this view during early steps and on full-resolution space-time tiles during later steps, so no network processes a whole frame at full resolution. Our sampler, ReTile, addresses the second reason. We use randomized grid shifting at every sampling step along with re-noising, which acts as a stochastic gradient instead of computing overlapping tiles, so a single network pass per sampling step suffices. On its own, ReTile speeds up an existing global-context patch prior by 6-9x, and our prior adds a further 3-5x speedup. TesseraFlow reconstructs about one 256x256 frame per second, 20-367x faster than patch and latent video priors, and can match or exceed the best generative baseline on cardiac, brain, and abdominal MRI and lung CT, at a fraction of its GPU time.

This commentary offers first-principles perspectives across four application domains: anomaly detection in clinical trial data, real-time monitoring and adaptive trial designs, biomarker-based enrichment, and virtual and digital twins. For each domain, we articulate the underlying inferential problem, the contribution of AI and ML relative to established statistical methods, and the risks that statisticians, data scientists, and clinical developers must actively manage. Across these domains, a unifying theme emerges: AI and ML methods may succeed locally while failing to satisfy the broader statistical assumptions required for valid clinical and regulatory decisions. We argue that a clearly defined context of use, relevant statistical assumptions, and consequences of the intended decision should guide the evaluation. For treatment-effect questions, the ICH E9(R1) estimand framework provides a useful organizing principle. Responsible use of AI in clinical development requires not restraint, but rigor: the inferential discipline necessary to make these technologies reproducible, interpretable, and defensible in the service of patients.

##### **When Can an AI Agent Serve as a Model of Human Psychopathology?**
2611.28405v1 by Marc-Andre Schulz, Pauline Scheuber

Language models and AI agents are increasingly described in psychiatric terms, such as anxious, depressed, addicted or helpless. A recent study even proposed language models as experimental systems for psychiatry and as a platform for piloting psychological treatments. We ask when an experiment on an AI agent can provide evidence about a specified human psychiatric process. A claim to such evidence has three parts. Something changed in the agent, and the agent's history caused the change. Nothing other than a process shared with people explains that change. The change matches one theory of the human process and not that theory's rivals. Psychiatry and its neighbouring fields have an evidence standard for each part, from the diagnostic manuals' rule for induced states, from clinical test scores and from animal models. We translate each standard into a criterion that a machine study can meet. For a language model the most critical alternative to a shared process is extrapolation from its training text, which already contains descriptions of how people respond to trauma and how they answer questionnaires. We show how to probe that alternative. Further, we audited forty-three published studies that apply psychiatric terms to a machine or induce a comparable state in one. Most compared the machine with an identical copy that had not gone through the history that induced the state. None excluded extrapolation from training text. Three tested a psychiatric theory against its rivals, and none of the three tests was conclusive. Until better evidence exists, a treatment pilot on a language model is best read as the model's forecast of patient response, and not as an experiment on a model organism. Evidence for a model organism will come from states induced by what happens to the machine, from experiments on its internal workings, and from tests on which rival theories disagree.

2611.27425v1 by Ahaan Dabholkar, Harshvardhan Patel, Michalis Polychronakis, Saurabh Bagchi

Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can reason directly about the weights of other neural networks. Given only a natural-language objective, an LLM can inspect a model's weights, understand what they compute, and edit them directly without gradient-based optimization. This capability, termed weight-space programming (WSP), has been used to improve model performance on math reasoning, medical QA and other benchmarks. We propose TrojanMaxxing, a novel strategy to backdoor vision-language models (VLMs), by reframing it as a WSP task. We use Claude Opus 5.5 and Mythos 5.2 agents to backdoor VLMs without any optimization or training methods. The agents inspect the model weights, apply targeted edits, and co-design an imperceptible visual trigger that activates the backdoor. We then study the agents' strategies and present a thorough evaluation of the trojaned models on various benchmark datasets. TrojanMaxxing achieves attack success rates of 93%-100% on content moderation and 78%-100% on OCR while maintaining clean accuracy. Perhaps most consequentially, we show that injecting backdoors in a training-free manner is possible by simply, "asking an agent to do it".

##### **Evidence sufficiency assessment enables cost-effective review allocation in on-premise clinical AI agents**
2611.27390v1 by Yiwen Qiu, Che Sun, Shu Shen, Wentao Wang

On-premise clinical AI agents must auto-accept high-confidence diagnoses, deferring others to clinician review, yet token probabilities and self-reported confidence miss errors, while more reliable five-run consistency incurs fivefold compute. We propose training-free decision-point evidence sufficiency assessment and a three-tier sequential triage protocol. Masking reasoning and diagnosis, the same 4B model scores sufficiency from objective evidence via prior-debiased probabilities over five ordered levels. Calibration freezes a nominal top-50% threshold, selecting single-run, five-run consistency, or full-deferral tiers by whether the one-sided 95% Wilson lower confidence bound of pass rate among accepted cases meets the 95% clinical target line. Across 1,605 MIRA-v2, CDM, VivaBench, and in-hospital liver malignancy primary cohort cases, MIRA-v2 single-run top-50% pass rate reached 98.91%, matching five-run consistency, saving ~80% compute; CDM achieved 96.00% test attainment by escalating to the five-run consistency tier; cross-specialty, missing-imaging, and combined enriched cohorts entered the full-deferral tier, capping calibration false-certification rate at 0.00%-3.50%.

##### **Can Reasoning and Image Description Enhance Yes/No Visual Mathematical Understanding?**
2611.27302v1 by Guangcheng Song, Weichuan Wang, Linqi Song

Large Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on general visual tasks, yet their ability to understand mathematical images remains limited. This paper investigates whether explicit reasoning and textual image descriptions can enhance VLMs' performance on binary yes/no visual mathematical questions. To achieve this, we curated a paired evaluation set to benchmark prominent open-source VLMs under varying prompt and input conditions. In particular, we leverage Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting and use textual descriptions, either replacing or augmenting visual inputs, to isolate and evaluate mathematical comprehension. Our results show that explicit reasoning improves performance, especially in paired consistency accuracy, while textual image descriptions do not consistently help and may degrade performance. These findings suggest that reasoning is important for stable visual mathematical judgement, whereas description-based intermediates require more careful design.

##### **DermKG: A Concept-Centric Knowledge Graph for Interpretable Skin Disease Diagnosis from Dermatological Morphology**
2611.27283v1 by Cheng Huang, Yanmei Li, Mao Lin

Dermatologists describe lesions with a standardized morphological lexicon, which makes concept-based models attractive for interpretable skin disease diagnosis. How much diagnostic information these concepts actually carry, however, is rarely quantified. We present DermKG, a concept-centric knowledge graph built on the expert-annotated SkinCon subset of Fitzpatrick17k (3,230 images, 48 concepts, 113 diseases). DermKG has 3,452 nodes of 8 types and 19,365 edges of 11 relation types, and encodes disease concept profiles, concept co-occurrence, a three-level diagnostic hierarchy, and 20 clinical morphology rules, all of which are supported by the training data (15 strong, 5 moderate). An information-ceiling analysis shows that the 3,230 lesions collapse onto only 813 distinct concept vectors, bounding concept-only top-1 accuracy at 38.9\% for diseases and 77.6\% for the nine categories. Within this bound, DermKG-Net, a graph neural network that scores lesions against disease prototypes and aggregates them along the hierarchy, raises category-level balanced accuracy from 0.257 to 0.411 over the strongest of 11 baselines (paired bootstrap difference $+0.150$, 95\% CI $[0.073, 0.231]$) and more than doubles minority-category recall. Ablations show that most of this gain comes from the hierarchical prototype objective, with a smaller contribution from graph structure. DermKG further yields rule-based reasoning chains and concept-level explanations for individual predictions.

##### **GEG-SAM: Gaze Exploration-Guided Segment Anything Model for Weakly Supervised Polyp Segmentation**
2611.27183v1 by Ao Wang, Lei Zhang, Lituan Wang, Zhenwei Zhang, Xiangbao Gong, Zhiyi Zhang, Shunyao Wang, Xianglei Yuan, Zizhou Wang

Endoscopist gaze offers an unobtrusively acquired record of lesion-oriented visual exploration during routine clinical inspection, providing relevant supervision for tasks. However, existing methods treat fixations as isolated spatial prompts directly associated with the target, overlooking the fact that natural gaze records a continuous visual search process in which fixations are temporally connected, spatially organized, and coupled with the visual content. Interpreting each fixation independently based only on its local visual content overlooks the temporal evolution and spatial organization of the gaze trajectory, whereas modeling relations among fixations alone cannot reveal the visual semantics of the regions they attend to. To this end, we propose the Gaze Exploration-Guided Segment Anything Model (GEG-SAM), a principled weakly supervised framework designed to explicitly exploit structured gaze relations and their corresponding visual cues to modulate SAM for polyp segmentation. Specifically, GEG-SAM captures structured gaze relations by modeling the temporal dynamics and spatial topology of gaze exploration. It then associates these relational representations with fixation-aligned visual context to derive gaze-conditioned tokens, which are further injected into the frozen SAM encoder to modulate its pretrained image features. In this way, GEG-SAM effectively exploits the rich information embedded in gaze exploration to guide SAM toward lesion-oriented segmentation. Extensive experiments on five public polyp segmentation datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, improving the mean Dice by 9.30 percentage points over the strongest weakly supervised baseline.

##### **How Does SAM-3 Adapt? Capacity, Update Geometry, and Domain Transfer**
2611.27142v1 by Allan Kazakov, Abdurrahman Javat, Duygu Cakir

Promptable segmentation foundation models are increasingly used in specialized domains, but it remains unclear how SAM-3 should be fine-tuned when adapting it beyond its original training distribution. Across these datasets, compact parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods often match or approach Full Fine-Tuning in DSC and IoU, suggesting that strong in-domain adaptation does not always require updating the full model. However, this performance cannot be explained by nominal adapter rank or trainable parameter count alone. To investigate why different methods perform similarly, we analyze their update spectra and their behavior under LoveDA Urban/Rural transfer. Our SVD analysis shows that Full Fine-Tuning updates are spectrally compressible. The domain-transfer study further shows that methods with similar in-domain performance can respond differently under distribution shift. Together, these results suggest that SAM-3 adaptation is often capacity-saturated in practice, while competitive fine-tuning arises through method-specific update paths rather than a single low-rank approximation to Full Fine-Tuning.

##### **Learning 3D MRI Representations with Generative Adversarial Networks**
2611.27057v1 by Sujeet Lahane, Sapna Mishra, Akash Suryavanshi, Tapan Gandhi, Bharat Biswal

Learning representations of visual images is a challenging task. It requires extracting abstract patterns from high-dimensional data without explicit supervision. This becomes pronounced with 3D MR imaging due to sparse features, higher dimensional inputs, and smaller datasets. Many state-of-the-art representation learning algorithms successfully rely on transformers and diffusion-based architectures. However, these methods struggle with smaller datasets. In effect, solution methods relax the problem by slicing 3D volumes and solving the problem for 2D medical imaging, resulting in a loss of spatial 3D information. We present R2RG, a representation learning method based on Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), with Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) honing the reconstruction task. Our key insights lie in exploiting architectural biases in 3D CNNs, combined with data-efficient auto-encoding GANs and a composite loss function to enhance reconstruction fidelity and stabilize training. Our experiments on three standard datasets and a locally collected dataset show that R2RG not only achieves superior performance but also generalizes to heterogeneous and smaller datasets. Furthermore, representations learned with R2RG outperform those learned with contrastive-based representation learning methods on Parkinson's and Alzheimer's disease classification. Our work demonstrates that reconstruction-based representation learning is a promising direction for 3D medical imaging under limited data availability.

##### **SecretAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Privacy Protection against Vision Language Models**
2611.26936v1 by Minda Zhao, Yue Zhang, Mengyu Wang

Vision-language models (VLMs) can infer private information from shared images, including locations, personally identifiable information (PII), and personal attributes such as age and sex, even from medical images. ##### **MedicalRSI: Medical Recursive Self-Improving Agents via Clinical Model-Harness Co-Evolution**
2611.26806v1 by Ziqing Wang, Hamid Palangi, Lili Zhao, Kaize Ding

Medical agents built on large language models (LLMs) have advanced rapidly, yet most stay fixed once deployed and do not keep using their experience to improve later attempts. As a result, the same kind of error can recur across patients. To address this, existing self-improvement methods update an agent's weights or its medical harness from its own experience, but their improving strategy stays fixed and never learns which changes actually help, which caps how much the agent can improve. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) instead improves both the agent and its improving strategy. However, achieving RSI for medical agents is challenging, because clinical workflows rarely come with a reference harness, and a change that fixes early errors may not fix the problems the agent meets later. We propose MedicalRSI, an RSI framework built on Clinical Model-Harness Co-Evolution, in which one model plays two roles. The Solver is the medical agent itself, and the Improver is its learnable improving strategy, which writes a medical harness from the agent's failures. Each harness is rewarded by its gain over acting without it, and both roles are trained together, so the Improver keeps adapting to the problems the Solver has not yet solved. On multiple benchmarks and LLM backbones, MedicalRSI outperforms existing context-level and weight-level self-improvement methods and transfers well to unseen workflows, new LLMs and new databases, a step toward medical agents that keep improving.

Readouts of the tumor immune microenvironment (TIME), such as CD8+ T-cell density, intra-epithelial infiltration and the Ki67 index, combine tissue architecture from H&E with cell phenotypes from multiplex immunofluorescence (mIF). Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) could automate these readouts with an explanation, but two gaps remain. Pathology MLLMs read H&E alone, while these readouts need the cell phenotypes that mIF measures. And their reasoning has no step-level truth to be checked against, so R1-style reinforcement learning rewards only the final answer and leaves the reasoning unverified. Without the process reward, reinforcement learning barely improves on supervised fine-tuning (80.0% vs. 78.8%) and fewer stated quantities are correct; editing a decisive stated quantity changes the answer as implied in all 885 test questions. Without retraining, it keeps the highest average accuracy on an external cohort imaged with CODEX (76.4% vs. 67.3%) and on head and neck cancer, a cancer type absent from training (76.7% vs. 72.5%).

##### **Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher**
2611.26598v1 by Yang Meng, Zixin Ding, Robert L. Grossman, Yuxin Chen

Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.

##### **Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding**

But do these agents use the resulting evidence effectively? To answer this question, we need a benchmark that separates evidence use from segmentation quality, spans multiple medical image types, and allows the same mask to be presented in different ways. Existing medical VQA datasets do not jointly provide these properties. We therefore construct Mask-Evidence MedVQA, a 2.5K-example benchmark spanning four image types, where each question is paired with an answer-relevant ground-truth mask and multiple possible renderings. Our study reveals two limitations of direct tool use. First, evidence presentation matters. Different renderings of the same fixed mask can substantially change model behavior. Second, masks introduce an evidence trade-off. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. Although EO did not necessarily lead to harm, recurring patterns often co-occurred with harmful outcomes, suggesting value as risk indicators. Open-source LLMs satisfactorily detected 12 of 14 EO behaviors in at least one configuration. Qualitative analysis revealed expectations of care, repeated support seeking, dependency, distress, and reduced confidence in personal judgment. We argue for graduated safety interventions tailored to patterns and trajectories of EO-related behavior.

##### **Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning for Mammography Vision--Language Models**
2611.25418v1 by Zhengbo Zhou, Dooman Arefan, Shandong Wu

Medical vision--language models (VLMs) often need to identify small, subtle lesions within high-resolution images containing extensive normal anatomy and redundant visual information. Visual-token compression improves efficiency, but preserving salient regions alone can discard the context needed for diagnostic interpretation. This context can extend across images: in bilateral mammography, the opposite breast provides a patient-specific anatomical reference. Yet retaining such evidence does not ensure that the model uses it effectively. We propose Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning (BESR), which connects evidence preservation with comparative assessment through a bilateral-guided token selector and a bilateral reward. Under a shared token budget, the selector jointly prioritizes lesion appearance and its local and bilateral context, while the reward encourages assessments that benefit from the preserved comparison. Experiments on a bilateral dataset constructed from CBIS-DDSM and on VinDr-Mammo demonstrate improved evidence retention and diagnostic performance under constrained visual-token budgets. Inference benchmarking further shows reduced prefill latency.

##### **MADJEPA: Enhanced Multi-Stage Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture for Medical Anomaly Detection**
2611.25194v2 by Ioana-Sabina Stoian, Florinel-Alin Croitoru, Vlad Hondru, Andreea Iuliana Ionescu, Andrei Paun, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Anomaly detection in medical images is a complex task, requiring the development of a robust model using only healthy patient data. Without access to labeled data, modern approaches typically employ self-supervised learning to obtain a model of normality. Since world models promise to build strong internal representations of their environment, we consider them as suitable candidates for the medical anomaly detection environment, namely medical investigations of healthy patients. To this end, we propose MADJEPA, a medical anomaly detector based on an enhanced multi-stage joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). We design a multi-stage training pipeline to produce a robust detector: (1) image-based JEPA (I-JEPA) training aims to learn a strong representation of normal training data; (2) target encoder distillation aims to modulate over-generalization of I-JEPA representations to abnormal data; (3) pseudo-anomaly classification aims to calibrate anomaly predictions by learning a tight boundary around the normal data manifold. To demonstrate our design, we carry out comprehensive experiments on six datasets covering various medical image domains (e.g. MRI, OCT, X-ray, histopathology), comparing our MADJEPA with numerous state-of-the-art methods. The results show that our carefully designed world model, MADJEPA, attains competitive results, ranking first on three datasets. Our ablation studies further confirm the utility of each training stage, indicating that I-JEPA alone is not sufficient to obtain competitive results in medical anomaly detection. We publicly release our code at https://github.com/Sabina085/madjepa.

##### **Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws**
2611.24877v1 by Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

##### **When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? A Controlled Study of Dialectal Adaptation for Québec French**
2611.24699v1 by Eeham Khan, Richard Khoury, Leila Kosseim

Continual pre-training (CPT) is widely used to adapt pretrained language models to dialectal and non-standard language varieties, yet it remains unclear when this additional training is necessary, what drives its benefits, and whether aggregate evaluation scores adequately capture its effects. In this paper, we present a controlled study of CPT for Québec French language understanding across nine LLMs spanning different families and scales. Using a budget-matched protocol of 30M tokens per experiment, our results show that the benefit of CPT depends strongly on the starting model's existing competence in the target variety. When CPT is beneficial, the improvements are driven more by corpus composition than by data volume, with literary text accounting for most of the gain. Paired Québec and standard French evaluations further show that CPT primarily improves expression comprehension rather than grammatical competence, and that corpus register determines which regional expressions are acquired. These gains also involve capability-specific trade-offs, including declines in reading comprehension that aggregate scores can conceal. Overall, these findings show that CPT for dialectal adaptation should not be treated as a uniformly beneficial default. Instead, adaptation strategies should begin with a careful assessment of the base model's existing competence, accompanied by source-level data analysis and task-level evaluation.

##### **Self-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Better-Structured Medical Explanations in Small Language Models**
2611.24650v2 by Prakhar Mishra, Bhavya Yajush Awasthi,  Tanuja,  Nishant

Small language models (SLMs) can run on commodity hardware and support privacy-preserving, on-premises clinical deployment, but their medical explanations are often short and poorly structured compared with large language models. Recent supervised approaches such as Meerkat transfer chain-of-thought reasoning into 7B-8B backbones, yet their training objective does not directly target the style or organization of the generated rationales. Building on the instruction-tuned Mistral-7B backbone, we investigate whether a lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) step can improve explanation structure without human preference labels. We apply Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with LoRA adaptation to 2000 Meerkat-derived medical prompts, guided by a simple self-supervised reward that favors longer, lexically diverse, and well-punctuated responses while penalizing very short ones. Training for about 170 PPO steps on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU yields a stable increase in the average reward. Quantitative and qualitative comparisons show that the RL-tuned model produces longer, more organized, multi-point explanations while preserving the underlying medical content. We discuss the promise and the limitations of self-rewarded RL for shaping the style of medical explanations.
